# Alastair Law

**Alastair Law** ("Al", Yeovil, England) built the Paradox **"Little Jim"** almost entirely single-handed, launching her at Poole Harbour in April 2003. A professional CAD engineer with an engineer's turn of mind, he left one of the fullest and most detailed records in the archive — spanning construction technique, rigging, trailering, sailing performance, and design philosophy — and the fullest firsthand cruising accounts of any Paradox owner. Paradox was his first "real" boat, after only an 8 ft pram dinghy — built about 25 years earlier, by his own later recollection — and a school canoe.[1] Seventeen years on, in May 2020, he presented "Little Jim" to the Amateur Yacht Research Society — a mature retrospective that corroborates and extends his earlier accounts; see [Little Jim — a 2020 retrospective](/boats/little-jim.md#a-2020-retrospective-the-ayrs-talk).[2]

He signs every message "Al, Yeovil, England," and closed many with "Hoping for calm nights" — by his own explanation, because he can't get to sleep if the water is all lumpy.[3] He wrote under the byline Alastair Law for both the Dinghy Cruising Association *Bulletin* and a *Messing About in Boats* cruising account.[4][5]

## Little Jim

Building began in August 2001 and reached a full launch over Easter 2003, done single-handed apart from moving full plywood sheets and turning the hull over. A defining constraint was the workspace: a **garage only about 2 ft longer than the finished boat**, with only 2 ft clearance on either side — an early data point on the modest workshop a Paradox build can require, and the reason turning the hull took two people and improvised roller skates. The build produced the archive's fullest single build-cost accounting, restated more than once as a full line-item breakdown (timber, plywood, epoxy, insulation foam, Lexan, and the sail) answering newcomers' recurring "how can it cost that much" questions, and again as an itemized bill of plywood sheets by thickness, glass cloth yardage, and epoxy gallons for a builder without a set of plans yet — see [build effort and workspace — cost](/construction/build-effort.md#cost).[6] The full build, launch, and cruising narrative lives on [Little Jim](/boats/little-jim.md); the entries below summarize the durable contributions and cross-link to where each is told in full.

## Construction contributions

- **Reading the plans: a Drawing #12 correction, and a first-time builder's backlog of questions, answered.** The spar drawing, Drawing #12, carries stray dimension-arrow marks that, once painted out, leave the sheet's dimensioning correct and its two views easier to tell apart. A first-time builder's full backlog of plans-reading questions resolves as follows: buy glass on 50 in-wide rolls to lay single join-free sheets on the bottom, sides, and deck; the stateroom hatch can use the same piano hinge as the cabin hatches, though a webbing hinge would suit mud and grit better; shelves mount to the bin sides with a plain epoxy butt joint and fillets; the page-5 metal angle is just a bent inch-long piece of stainless; and most of the plywood parts missing from the plans' 10-sheet cutting diagram can be found on already labeled sheets or from left-over scrap, needing at most one extra spare sheet.[7][8] Anything built into the forward stateroom must fit back out through its opening, since it goes in before the deck does; built to the stock plan, the woodwork there still comes out again in a two-minute job, making it easy to try different layouts (the stock central partition can be kept while reducing its height).[9] The page-5 metal-angle fitting is often misread: the fold runs across the strap's width, not down its length, forming a small bracket that holds the bin face's middle down to the floor panel.[10] For a builder needing to fit the vent trunk sides/front, breast hook, and both rudder pieces out of a single 18 mm sheet, a specific space-saving layout works — flipping the bottom rudder piece about a horizontal axis and sliding it inside bulkhead 3's outline, which incidentally also solves the rudder "nose" otherwise falling off the sheet's edge; cutting newspaper templates lets a layout be tested by hand without CAD software.[11] See [building plans — reading the plans: a first-time builder's backlog of questions](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions).
- **A pre-build 3D CAD assembly of the whole boat.** Before cutting any wood, "assembled" a Paradox in the 3D CAD software used professionally at work — well worth the effort, since it sorted out a lot of the queries about how the plans' parts fit together. See [building plans — a March 2012 proposal: 3D CAD models to supplement the paper plans](/references/building-plans.md#a-march-2012-proposal-3d-cad-models-to-supplement-the-paper-plans).[257]
- **A floor-lowering case against raising the cabin for headroom.** Lowering the floor suits more headroom better than raising the structure above it, since a higher head position forces the boom up too, in turn calling for a smaller sail or a taller mast — and a taller mast won't stow inside the boat for trailering, while also raising the sail's center of pressure. See [aluminum and steel Paradox (speculative) — a raised cabin, canopy, and cockpit-height ideas](/design/ideas/aluminum-construction-idea.md#a-raised-cabin-canopy-and-cockpit-height-ideas).
- **Bulkhead curvature traced from finished beams, and free-electron ballast.** A bulkhead's exact top curvature matters little either way, since most beams and bulkheads get fine-tuned again once the deck itself is fitted; see [deck and cabin beam lamination — drawing bulkhead curvature from the finished beams, rather than the plans](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#drawing-bulkhead-curvature-from-the-finished-beams-rather-than-the-plans). On pairing gel-cell battery ballast with an electric outboard: going electric lets batteries substitute for lead, but an autohelm is unnecessary — Paradox was designed to sail without constant attention to the helm, and a course check only about every five minutes suffices as part of an ordinary look-out routine; see [weight, ballast, and displacement — gel-cell batteries as ballast, an electric outboard, and the Bird Watcher II precedent](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#gel-cell-batteries-as-ballast-an-electric-outboard-and-the-bird-watcher-ii-precedent).
- **Chine-log construction, defended and cautioned.** The plans' chine-log method is preferable to a proposed taped-seam substitution: taped seam is workable but less rugged given Paradox's heavy displacement — see [chine log vs. taped-seam construction](/construction/chine-log-vs-taped-seam.md). A solid bent chine log is "by far the most difficult part of the assembly"; **laminating the chine log** is strongly preferable to bending it solid, and the notch should be cut horizontal-only until the sides are fitted, to keep the corner that locates the side panel; see [notching the stem for chine logs and sheer clamps](/construction/stem-notching.md). On his own boat he in fact fitted a single unlaminated piece rather than following that advice, a dry test-fit's stored energy leaving him too wary of removing it again to do more than force epoxy in around it in place; see [chine log vs. taped-seam construction — a 2011 recurrence](/construction/chine-log-vs-taped-seam.md#a-2011-recurrence-two-more-builders-firsthand-installation-accounts).[242] Answering the same question again four years later, gave essentially the same account unchanged.[276]
- **Chine runner shaping, and a warning on foil-faced foam.** One workable method shapes a chine runner with a single batten filled to shape with scrap wood and epoxy, an alternative to pre-cut solid blocks; 25 lb lead ballast bricks are impractical to unbolt and shift at sea given the disassembly and re-securing a knockdown would demand, so shifting a crew member further forward is the simpler trim fix; foil-faced foam insulation blocks radio signals such as GPS inside the cabin; see [chine runner construction](/chine-runners/construction.md#casting-lead-ballast-into-the-runners). A full-length pre-shaped strake is almost impossible to bend and hold while the epoxy cures; the practical alternative is cutting it into shorter pieces glued in as a three-dimensional jigsaw, then grinding the result fair to templates with an angle grinder. See [chine runner construction — templates, epoxy fill, and a hand plane: a fourth working method](/chine-runners/construction.md#method-4-templates-epoxy-fill-and-a-hand-plane). Runners shaped freehand by "just waving an angle grinder at them till they looked about right" can still come out fine even against a rebuild elsewhere that corrected width, location, and shape to within 1-2 mm — see [Whisper — chine runners rebuilt after the shakedown cruise](/boats/whisper.md#chine-runners-rebuilt-after-the-shakedown-cruise). The same block-and-fill method transfers to sheet metal: bottom ply fitted proud of the sides, trimmed to profile, chine-runner wedge filled with wood blocks and epoxy, then shaped with a sanding disc on an angle grinder — the same approach should work in metal, at the cost of losing a little material at the radius. The curve's real purpose is likely protecting the edge of the bottom's fiberglass laminate rather than anything hydrodynamic. See [chine runners — how they work](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md#the-chine-runner-curve-cosmetic-fabrication-choice-or-hydrodynamic-feature).
- **Why the chine log's drawn bevel isn't uniform, and how bevel order resolves in practice.** The chine log's sheet-5 shape shows the bevel drawn correctly at the log's ends but not at the middle, since the logs and bulkheads are meant to be faired together once installed rather than pre-cut to a perfect bevel and simply assembled; an extra triangular section at the chine log's bottom follows from the bottom-panel measurement not being vertical, so some angle is unavoidable there, but given the assembly sequence it makes little practical difference. One working method for the chine-log/sheer-clamp bevel order: cut to the sheet-5 profiles, spring the sides into position once the bulkheads are up, then trim bottom and deck to fit; bulkhead bevels taken from sheet 2 still need real adjustment at trial assembly, so oversize cleats cut to the true angle only at that stage work better, and working entirely in millimetres with a metric tape beats converting back and forth. A bulkhead's plywood and cleat share the same curve and bevel; an alternative sequence — cut plywood to size, let cleats overlap the edge, and cut bevels only once trial assembly reveals the true angle — also works. See [hull assembly sequence — fit at trial assembly rather than pre-cutting a perfect bevel](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#fit-at-trial-assembly-rather-than-pre-cutting-a-perfect-bevel), [hull assembly sequence — bevel order for the chine log and sheer clamp](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#bevel-order-for-the-chine-log-and-sheer-clamp-cut-before-or-after-installing), and [hull assembly sequence — the bevel and cleat relationship](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#clarifying-the-bevel-and-cleat-relationship-and-a-rebuild-it-differently-alternative).
- **Deck and cabin beams.** The deck is a **parabola, not a radius**; all the beams can be produced from the sheet-7 and sheet-10 curves by a mold-and-laminate method, and **bulkhead #3 needs no side cleats** — see [deck and cabin beam lamination](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md) and [hull assembly sequence](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md). A beam #1/centerline shortfall later isolated by other builders can go unnoticed amid the many other small gaps a workboat-finish build needs filling; see [deck and cabin beam lamination — shimming deck beam #1](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#shimming-deck-beam-1-and-its-neighbor-to-fair-the-centerline). A couple of millimetres' beam-lamination inaccuracy either way will, almost certainly, make no difference to the finished boat; see [deck and cabin beam lamination — the Build Manual's "Deck Beam Template" and "Laminating Jigs" installment](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#the-build-manuals-deck-beam-template-and-laminating-jigs-installment).
- **A yuloh built from Douglas Fir** — the species used throughout "Little Jim" for anything not marine plywood; see [yuloh and stowed-rig retention — wood for the yuloh](/components/yuloh.md#wood-for-the-yuloh).
- **A yuloh pivot socket seated directly in thickened epoxy.** Rather than a machined tufnol or Delrin block, drilled a hole a few millimetres wider than the pivot ball, filled it with thickened epoxy, then re-drilled through the cured epoxy to the ball's own size — a socket whose depth can be adjusted at will simply by filling and re-drilling again, needing refurbishment only about every four or five years; also confirmed the design's own pivot pin is built with an offset from the start. See [yuloh — ball-joint versus oarlock, and pivot-socket wear over time](/components/yuloh.md#ball-joint-versus-oarlock-and-pivot-socket-wear-over-time).[264]
- **Transom crown, and a looser view on bevel-fitting.** A transom can carry a slight, asymmetric crown, sized off the plan sheet showing it curved; exact bevels are not worth fussing over ahead of time — shaping them freehand with an angle grinder at trial assembly works better — "it's too much fun to sail to spend forever building it" — see [hull assembly sequence](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#transom-and-bulkhead-4-crown-and-fitting-bevels-at-trial-assembly). For bulkhead-member angles, the lift-the-angle-from-the-drawing method can leave some angles wrong, needing padding and re-cutting; planing bevels after assembly may be easier — though neither method needs great precision, since epoxy fixes the mistakes; see [hull assembly sequence — finding bulkhead-member angles from the drawings](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#finding-bulkhead-member-angles-from-the-drawings).
- **The strength case for coastal cruising, and the rudder pintle as evidence.** A boat built for serious coastal cruising arguably needs to be stronger than an offshore boat, since it must survive direct contact with reefs and beaches that a true offshore design is meant to avoid — Paradox's unusually robust 15 mm rudder pintle is a concrete example; see [blue-water suitability](/design/blue-water-suitability.md#structural-strength-as-the-real-limit-not-distance-from-shore).
- **A lightened alternative to structural tank tops.** The plans' structural hinged tank tops are not strictly necessary; when converting from water to lead ballast, cutting the tops back 3 in from the dividers and fitting removable hatches instead trades a small stiffness loss for good access; see [water tank construction and lining — structural hinged tank tops](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#structural-hinged-tank-tops-and-a-lightened-alternative).
- **The bulkhead side curve's purpose, and a cleat-extension fix for a missed one.** The plans' small stiffening curve along a bulkhead's side edge exists to resist oil-canning; a bulkhead already glued without it can still be corrected by extending the cleats over the missing edge and filling the gap with epoxy at assembly, since cured epoxy is stronger than the plywood it replaces — a philosophy he credits with making "Little Jim" herself, half in jest, stronger than the as-designed boat. See [panel layout and nesting — bulkhead side curves](/construction/panel-layout.md#bulkhead-side-curves) and [Little Jim — confirming the bulkhead side curve's purpose](/boats/little-jim.md#confirming-the-bulkhead-side-curves-purpose-and-the-philosophy-that-mistakes-end-up-epoxied-stronger).
- **Early panel-nesting dimensions, from the first cut list.** Within days of the wood's delivery, laid out bulkhead-2, the transom, and the side panels together on the sheet, resolving their overlaps with an aft base of 28 mm and a forward base of 115 mm to nest the parts efficiently. See [panel layout and nesting](/construction/panel-layout.md).[12]
- **A plans-layout tight-fit, confirmed.** The two side decks and two cabin end panels cannot all come from a single 4 ft-wide ply sheet as drawn — a re-measured build confirmed the tight fit (foredeck max width 3 ft 11 in, exceeding 4 ft with the crown) — see [panel layout and nesting](/construction/panel-layout.md).
- **Materials and finish notes.** The plans' millimetre values are metric conversions of Matt Layden's inch sizes (see [panel layout](/construction/panel-layout.md)). Hulls need not be glassed above the waterline where marine ply, a good finish, and alongside work are rare, and UK epoxy suppliers have been unwilling to certify their products for water tanks (plasticiser leaching), favoring the tanks as ballast-only (see [water tank lining](/construction/water-tank-lining.md)). Tufnol is a candidate rudder-bearing material, and skinned foam panels work for polystyrene flotation — see [rudder-bearing materials](/construction/rudder-bearing-materials.md) and [removable foam flotation panels](/construction/removable-foam-panels.md). "Little Jim"'s own lower rudder fitting was built from a block of Tufnol — see [casting a glass lower rudder gudgeon — technique](/construction/rudder-bearing-materials.md#casting-a-glass-lower-rudder-gudgeon-technique).
- **A split-copper-pipe rudder-edge repair, and a furling-spindle pipe stress-tested by a gybe.** Worn glass on the rudder's leading edge after a full season can be reinforced by band-sawing a length of 15 mm copper water pipe in two lengthwise, annealing one half to make it easy to form round the rudder's front and bottom, then epoxy-riveting it on through countersunk holes and fairing it smooth — a repair that held up well. The same 15 mm split copper pipe serves elsewhere too, between the furling spindle and the deck, sitting mostly in tension so a flimsy split end matters little; it gave no trouble in "Little Jim." An unplanned trial followed: sailing home the next day (a Force 5 following wind at 4-5 kn, then a broad reach at 3-4 kn with the sail 50% reefed), an unexpected gybe — no drama in a Paradox, with no boom/head interface to worry about — twisted the split end of that furling-spindle pipe badly, discovered only once the boat was back on the trailer. Hammering the bends out and soldering on doublers cut from leftover rudder repair tubing left the fitting stiffer, with its durability still to be proven. See [rudder-bearing materials](/construction/rudder-bearing-materials.md) and [furling system](/components/rig/furling.md).[13]
- **The case for epoxy over paint on a water-damaged hull.** Epoxy needs no surface preparation or interim sanding, must withstand heavy gear set down on it, and — left unpainted — lets a builder keep watching for renewed rot, unlike a painted surface; an epoxy-coated interior can still look pristine despite the anchor being dropped on it repeatedly. See [epoxy pre-coating technique — sealing a hull with prior water/rot damage](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#sealing-a-hull-with-prior-waterrot-damage-epoxy-vs-paint).
- **A padlock-through-the-hatch-rail fix against being locked in.** Rather than build a swiveling hasp against the risk of being locked in, drilling a hole through the hatch rail parallel to the deck, just in front of the closed hatch, lets a padlock pop through it to lock the boat from outside — see [hatch hardware and security](/construction/hatch-hardware.md#a-padlock-through-the-hatch-rail-alternative).
- **A formica wear-surface fix for a sticking hatch, and why a jammed hatch is not the real emergency.** Re-surfacing the hatch's foam-lined running edge with kitchen-worktop formica strip cured years of sticking at some cost to the foam seal's watertightness, after PTFE tape and parcel tape both proved short-lived. On the further worry of being trapped by a collision-jammed hatch: the runner's seven bolts each side are removable from inside, the boat floats even full of water regardless, and the real fire risk from the nearby cooker matters more than a stuck hatch — see [hatch hardware and security — a formica wear-surface fix for a foam-lined hatch](/construction/hatch-hardware.md#a-formica-wear-surface-fix-for-a-foam-lined-hatch) and [heavy-weather tactics — whether a closed cabin is a safety risk, or a comfort](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#whether-a-closed-cabin-is-a-safety-risk-or-a-comfort).[14]
- **Against a hatch-mounted view dome.** Judged one unnecessary, since the windows already give a good view, and doubted it could easily be made to line up with the designed sitting position. See [hatch — the plexiglass-dome question recurs](/components/hatch.md#the-plexiglass-dome-question-recurs-2018).[291]
- **A caution on comprehensive tool lists.** Seeing a complete tool inventory before starting a build risks putting a builder off, since a finished Paradox can be built with only about a tenth of the tools such a list names — a caution that a thorough planning checklist can overstate what a Paradox actually requires in practice. See [build effort and workspace — the Build Manual's "Preparations" installment](/construction/build-effort.md#the-build-manuals-preparations-installment-build-site-and-a-full-tool-inventory).
- **A cabin-only anchoring routine.** A frequent-anchoring technique — anchor stowed in the floor locker with a permanent bow line and aft snap hook, dropped and dug in under sail, then made fast and furled without ever leaving the cabin's protection — motivates a wish for a proper afterdeck anchor well. See [ground tackle and anchoring — a cabin-only anchoring routine, done entirely under sail](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-cabin-only-anchoring-routine-done-entirely-under-sail).
- **A yacht, not a dinghy, to sail.** Sailing a Paradox feels much more like sailing a yacht than a dinghy — true even for a sailor with scant experience before launching; see [Paradox as a first boat — sailing a Paradox feels more like sailing a yacht than a dinghy](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#sailing-a-paradox-feels-more-like-sailing-a-yacht-than-a-dinghy).
- **A laminated-strip alternative to bending sheet metal for the chine-runner curve.** Where a bronze sheet resists bending to the chine runner's radius, building up the required thickness from offcut strips laminated in layers (glued, riveted, brazed, or welded) lets enough material accumulate to grind the final curve — a metal-specific variant of the plywood block-and-fill method. See [chine runner construction — fabricating chine runners in steel, if the whole hull is steel](/chine-runners/construction.md#fabricating-chine-runners-in-steel-if-the-whole-hull-is-steel).
- **An epoxy-fume mask recommendation.** A cartridge filter mask with an ABEK-rated cartridge (bought from Buck & Hickman) stops the epoxy smell entirely and should be kept sealed in a plastic bag between uses to avoid premature filter exhaustion.[15] Bevels are best left with excess material until trial assembly reveals the true angle needed. See [bottom painting over epoxy — respirator advice](/construction/bottom-painting.md#stripping-old-bottom-paint-sanding-and-respirator-advice).
- **UK epoxy supplier confirmation, and a two-season condition report.** Both `epoxy-resins.co.uk` and SP Systems (Isle of Wight) are trouble-free UK epoxy sources. After a second full season of deliberately unfussy use, "Little Jim" needed attention at only two small bottom spots worn by repeated groundings, having followed the plans' "glass all over" scheme rather than any partial-coverage shortcut.[16] See [epoxy pre-coating technique — cloth-weight, canal-abrasion, and cloth-only-versus-glassed-topsides questions from a UK builder](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#cloth-weight-canal-abrasion-and-cloth-only-versus-glassed-topsides-questions-from-a-uk-builder).
- **Rear-locker divider geometry, and a stove-support fix.** A rear locker's curved floor can leave nominally-upright dividers meeting it at an acute angle, letting a bucket of heavy chain punch out its own bottom; fitting the dividers square to the floor instead avoids this, and a wedge-shaped block squares up the locker's stove-support axle bolt.[17][18] See [interior bin panels and hinged floor — rear-locker divider geometry, and where to stow heavy ground tackle](/components/interior.md#rear-locker-divider-geometry-and-where-to-stow-heavy-ground-tackle).
- **Hull-colour choice, and UV protection.** A UV-protective colour additive is not always necessary at UK ultraviolet levels — a plain-epoxy dinghy kit has shown no deterioration after two years — so matching whatever paint colour is already on hand beats spending sailing time deliberating; PVC floor tape makes a poor trim-line material.[19][20][21][22] See [topside and hull painting choices — choosing a hull colour, and why the epoxy's UV protection matters more than the shade](/construction/topside-painting.md#choosing-a-hull-colour-and-why-the-epoxys-uv-protection-matters-more-than-the-shade).
- **Commiseration and a pilot-hole fix.** A bottom-panel measuring error leaving a 6 in gap calls for "a large drink and a good night's sleep" more than anything else; a bent-bronze-ring-nail problem is solved by drilling larger pilot holes.[23][24] See [scarf joints — a dovetail-shaped lap joint after a bottom-panel measuring error](/construction/scarf-joints.md#a-dovetail-shaped-lap-joint-after-a-bottom-panel-measuring-error) and [fastening and gluing — pilot-hole sizing](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#bronze-ring-nails-bending-over-pilot-hole-sizing).
- **Drilling the boom's tack-end swivel-rod bore.** Clamp the boom horizontally in a vice and drill the tack-end hole freehand, oversize, then epoxy in a length of 15mm copper pipe (capped at its end) to give the swivel rod a proper bearing surface, allowing a small correction during the gluing itself; at the boom's other end, the sheet/topping-lift tang-bolt hole takes the same drill-oversize/epoxy-fill/drill-and-tap fix already established elsewhere, built stronger than seems necessary since an underbuilt tang can bend enough to prevent reefing.[255] See [boom-end tack line holes — drilling the tack end for the swivel-rod bearing](/construction/boom-end-tack-line-holes.md#drilling-the-tack-end-for-the-swivel-rod-bearing-and-a-further-tang-bolt-caution).
- **Cleat gluing orientation, read from the scale of the drawings.** For bulkhead 2's cleats (and other bulkheads), the larger of a cleat's two faces is settled by looking directly at sheet 4 rather than by a written rule; since the drawings are made accurately to scale, any single already-known dimension lets a builder calibrate a ruler against the sheet and read further undimensioned figures straight off it. See [panel layout and nesting — cleat gluing orientation](/construction/panel-layout.md#cleat-gluing-orientation-and-calibrating-a-scale-directly-off-the-drawings).
- **A structural-weight/ballast tradeoff, restated for a thinner-plywood question.** Answering a newcomer weighing whether Paradox really needs its specified panel thickness or could be built lighter in 9mm sheets: any weight saved from a lighter structure has to be made up as added lead ballast, so it is better design practice to keep a boat's weight where it does useful work. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a 2012 recurrence: the 9mm-substitution question restated, and a structural-weight/ballast tradeoff](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2012-recurrence-the-9mm-substitution-question-restated-and-a-structural-weightballast-tradeoff).[261]

- **The removable back window's swing direction.** A Paradox back window hinged at the top, swinging inward, could strike a sailor on the back of the head in an unexpected powerboat wash — a bottom-hinged, outward-swinging arrangement is safer. See [Little Cruiser — the removable back window](/matt-layden/little-cruiser.md#the-removable-back-window-design-rationale-and-paradox-builders-adaptation-ideas).
- **A lead-melting safety warning.** Melting down old lead pipe can be dangerous if the pipe holds trapped moisture: the moisture can flash to steam and fire a slug of molten lead out like a gun — a 6 in length of pipe once threw lead about 10 ft this way. Always point the pipe away from anyone nearby. See [Swamp Thing — rudder rebuild technique](/matt-layden/swamp-thing.md#rudder-rebuild-technique-beveled-lead-versus-screwed-lead-and-a-lead-melting-caution).
- **A constant-ballast bladder-tank idea.** Fitting flexible water bladders inside otherwise-watertight built-in tanks would let sea water in around the bladder as fresh water is drawn down from inside it, keeping total ballast weight constant through a cruise. See [water tank construction and lining — not using the built-in tanks at all](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#not-using-the-built-in-tanks-at-all-individual-containers-instead).
- **Bulkhead-1 access and anchor wells, declined.** Through-bolted deck fittings need bulkhead-1 access from below; a sealed "watertight" compartment tends to fill with water it then cannot drain, an argument against a flush anchor well on the same weight-forward and exposed-bow grounds that favor a stern-worked anchoring routine. See [interior bins and floor — design intent](/components/interior.md#design-intent-watertight-bulkhead-opening-is-optional) and [ground tackle and anchoring](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-flush-anchor-well-considered-and-declined-glen-maxwell). Two people can sit on the Paradox seat with the hatch open, though "a bit of a squeeze" underway. See [crew size and cabin fit](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#sitting-two-side-by-side-on-the-seat-hatch-open).
- **Reassurance on manhandling, and a cheap non-skid find.** Even a tired-out moving crew can safely leave the hull propped on rocks partway through a move, thanks to the 18 mm bottom's toughness; cheap rubberized stair-tread strip from Ikea works well as deck non-skid, and comes cheaply by the roll. See [build effort and workspace — kit-cut panels versus building from scratch](/construction/build-effort.md#workspace) and [non-skid deck finish — truck-bed and industrial coatings](/construction/non-skid-deck.md#truck-bed-and-industrial-coatings).
- **Ballast-tank fill/suction plumbing, in practice.** Filling or draining a side takes 5-10 minutes from a tap, longer by bilge pump, using deck fittings sourced at a boat jumble; forcing water in too fast once blew off one of his own inspection hatches, and sailing without the water ballast in light conditions has a noticeable effect on initial stability even with the full lead load aboard. See [transom deck plate — the fill/suction copper pipe: sizing, timing, and the fittings that connect to it](/construction/transom-deck-plate.md#the-fillsuction-copper-pipe-sizing-timing-and-the-fittings-that-connect-to-it).[25]
- **Answering a newcomer's rough-weather FAQ.** Heeling itself is what engages a chine runner, addressing a prospective builder's tacking question; the worst water he has met came aboard as solid green water over the cabin running a tide race with the tide against the wind, sailed with tiller and mainsheet both locked off and no water reaching the cabin despite audible flow through the vent trunk. See [heavy-weather tactics — a newcomer's rough-weather FAQ: tacking, pitchpoling, submarining, and lying to a drogue](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#a-newcomers-rough-weather-faq-tacking-pitchpoling-submarining-and-lying-to-a-drogue).[26]
- **Opened the "dingos" modification-philosophy debate.** Pointed the list to a Duckworks article on modifying a design and offered his own experience — tempted to modify while building, but glad in hindsight he built as drawn, since every change he had considered would have made the boat worse; a follow-up drew the distinction between an informed modification (which he has no argument with) and one made without real understanding. Full text and citation also at [paradox.md](/paradox.md#citations).[27][28]
- **An untried standing, forward-facing oar concept, and a first-season no-motor discipline on Little Jim.** Considered, but never tried, oarlock brackets fitted to the sides of the open hatch to allow standing, forward-facing rowing, addressing two yuloh drawbacks — holding the head to wind, and stopping/reversing at close quarters; separately recalled going without a motor his first season specifically to force himself to learn to sail, later adding a small electric trolling motor used only once sail and yuloh have both failed. Full text and citation also at [sculling and yuloh technique — whether Paradox can be rowed with conventional oars](/operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md#whether-paradox-can-be-rowed-with-conventional-oars).[29]
- **Insulation and draught exclusion over a heater, and a towel-in-the-vent trick.** Answering a suggestion that thicker insulation and better draught exclusion could substitute for a cabin heater, confirmed occasionally stuffing a towel into the ventilator to cut the cooling effect; already carries 2 in of foam and feels no need for more, having found "normal" sailing clothes comfortable even in weather that went below freezing overnight, and has never felt the need for a heater at all — a sailor unused to dinghy or small-yacht sailing tends to underestimate how much warmer it is simply out of the wind. See [cabin heating — heat storage and insulation alternatives to a heater](/operations/cabin-heating.md#heat-storage-and-insulation-alternatives-to-a-heater).[30]
- **Water ballast replaced with lead after leakage, and a webbing-hinge alternative to brass.** Originally built the water tanks to plan rather than convert them to storage, reasoning hatch covers could always be cut in later; has since replaced the water ballast with lead after leaks, while keeping the tank's air space nearly sealed to preserve flotation reserve, and would go back to water ballast if genuinely watertight, preferably metal, inspection hatches could be found; the plans' rear-sole-plate brass hinge picks up grit over time, and would be replaced with a webbing hinge if his own ever failed; endorsed a drop-in rear-cabin-trunk washboard as sound, provided it does not compromise the cabin's structure. A supply of hatches claiming to be genuinely air-tight, rather than merely weather-proof, was later sourced and due to be fitted the following winter to finally make the return to water ballast. See [water tank construction and lining — lead replacing leaked water ballast, and a hinge-material alternative to brass](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#lead-replacing-leaked-water-ballast-and-a-hinge-material-alternative-to-brass) and [boats/johanna.md](/boats/johanna.md#water-tanks-reconsidered-and-a-rear-sole-plate-question).[31][32]
- **A bottled drinking-water reserve nested inside the ballast tank.** Launching and recovering on a trailer every trip means wanting the tanks empty for towing, and many launch sites have no tap water at all, so the tanks often end up filled with sea water — ruling out ever drinking from them — and even a tap-water fill develops a slime layer inside after a few months regardless. The workaround: fill ordinary 2-litre soft-drink bottles with fresh water and pack them into the tank before topping the rest of the space up with ballast (sea) water, fishing bottles out as needed and topping back up with sea water to keep the ballast weight constant — free, easily cleaned, and simply replaced if they turn unpleasant. See [water tank construction and lining — a supplemental insert idea, and a proven bottled reserve nested in the ballast tank](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#a-supplemental-insert-idea-and-a-proven-bottled-reserve-nested-in-the-ballast-tank).[33]
- **Marine ply over exterior grade, and a minimal building floor.** Chose marine plywood over the plans' suggested exterior grade for his own boat to avoid extra maintenance down the line; a Paradox needs no more than a workbench and a couple of 4x4s on the ground to build on, with a flat patch of ground useful only for scarfing the side and bottom sheets, since the construction sequence is organized to be difficult to get wrong. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a July 2006 recurrence, and a recommendation for MDO plywood](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-july-2006-recurrence-and-a-recommendation-for-mdo-plywood).[34]
- **Free scrap lead, and what actually counts as "ballast."** Rather than buying lead, got 17 lead bricks (25 lb each) for the price of two pints of beer — tyre-fitters' shops routinely have buckets of old wheel-balance weights they pay to have taken away; now uses only 4 of the bricks, having progressively swapped lead out as he added other "useful" equipment aboard. Separately cautioned that a simple lead-weight figure is a slippery number to compare between boats, since it depends on whether water ballast and always-carried heavy gear (anchor, etc.) are counted as "ballast" or as stores/equipment — by different countings, his own boat could answer anywhere from 100 to 350 lb. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — ballast](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#ballast).[35][36]
- **A removable ballast fastening for access after the floors are fitted.** Drilled and tapped directly into the top of his own lead pigs and bolted on a plate anchored at its other end into the top of the chine log — not a rigid clamp, but the pigs show no sign of shifting and the fitting is expected to hold even inverted. Recommends leaving the ballast unfastened at first regardless of method, since pigs commonly get moved around during early sails to get a boat properly balanced. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — reaching the ballast fastenings after the floors are in](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#reaching-the-ballast-fastenings-after-the-floors-are-in).[37]
- **Underballasting is forgiving, not dangerous.** When asked about the minimum ballast needed to keep a "Power Paradox" (motor-only) stable, answered from direct experience that a boat does not suddenly become unstable from too little ballast — "Enuf" sailed significantly underballasted at her first launch with only increased tenderness to show for it, and on two occasions of his own has forgotten to fill the water ballast entirely, noticing only while moving on deck, not while sailing. See [Power Paradox — ballast for a motor-only hull](/design/ideas/power-paradox.md#ballast-for-a-motor-only-hull).[38]
- **An umbrella clamped to the hatch, and a similar fix seen elsewhere.** His own umbrella clamps onto the back edge of the sliding hatch — rarely used while sailing, but useful at anchor, letting him sit with his head outside the cabin while shedding rain, and only occasionally doubling as a sun shade; a similar wooden pole later spotted on "Zero" turned out to serve the same purpose. See [Aussie Paradox — a later modification: an umbrella pole on deck](/boats/aussie-paradox.md#a-later-modification-an-umbrella-pole-on-deck).[39][40]
- **The transom's overall height beyond drawing sheet #8's stated figure.** His own finished transom came out at 516 mm overall, and he cautioned against cutting the transom to size before the deck beams are actually fitted, since a beam that doesn't come out exactly to plan will leave a pre-cut transom not fitting. See [boom-end tack line holes and drilled-hole rot protection — the transom's overall height](/construction/boom-end-tack-line-holes.md#the-transoms-overall-height-drawing-sheet-8).[41]
- **Cabin-top and hatch-cover beam figures beyond sheet 10.** His own pencilled plans give the foremost cabin beam at 52 mm and the lower aft cabin beam at 75 mm, with both the forward bevel and the deck beam's own bevel shaped freehand with an angle grinder and sanding disc, sighted by eye standing in the roofless cabin against the beam's side-to-side curve; either the hatch-locking notch or the forward weather-seal notch can be routed from a full-sized beam or laminated in with the notch already in place, whichever tooling is easier — he laminated his own for lack of a router. See [deck and cabin beam lamination — cabin-top and hatch-cover beam bevels](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#cabin-top-and-hatch-cover-beam-bevels-open-questions-from-sheet-10).[42]
- **A folding bicycle stowed in the forward stateroom.** Considered an alternative arrangement in the forward stateroom to carry a Brompton folding bicycle aboard, reasoning a bicycle would open up sailing destinations otherwise out of reach on foot.[43]
- **A newcomer's cabin/hatch redesign ideas, answered from lived experience.** Answering the same "removable/openable cabin windows" idea Little Cruiser tried and Paradox dropped (see [Little Cruiser — the removable back window](/matt-layden/little-cruiser.md#the-removable-back-window-design-rationale-and-paradox-builders-adaptation-ideas)), could not think of a use for one: fine weather is spent outside, foul weather sends the crew inside regardless. On a longer sliding hatch or taller cabin roof, pointed to "Enuf"'s own mock-ups (which delayed her launch by a couple of months before her builder stayed with the plans) as the fullest record of anyone actually working through such a redesign. On stove ventilation, judged the cabin's normal ventilation adequate even shut up tight, and described stuffing a towel into the vent box on cold nights as his own way of cutting the airflow down. Having drawn up a long list of possible improvements before ever sailing the boat, concluded after sailing her that not one would actually have improved her, and limited his own changes to easier-access storage bins that make no difference to sailing. See [vent box](/components/vent-box.md).[44]
- **Glassing the deck before fitting the cabin.** A tire-cushioned hull roll-over, as used on a fellow builder's own boat, was confirmed as his own practice too; separately, glassing the deck before the cabin goes on lets the glass's edge sit tucked under the cabin structure rather than butting against it, judged the neater way to do it — a single sheet of glass cloth stretched over the whole deck at once, with the openings cut out only once the epoxy had started to cure. See [Little Jim — glassing the deck before the cabin, recalled](/boats/little-jim.md#glassing-the-deck-before-the-cabin-recalled-june-2008).[45]
- **A delamination caution from Faith's own hatch beam.** A plywood-laminated beam can delaminate within the ply itself, independent of how sound its epoxy joints are — observed on Faith's own forward hatch beam while cruising together through the Scilly Isles — arguing for fastening any laminated ply panel with nails or screws through it rather than trusting the panel's own internal glue lines. See [Faith — a laminated hatch beam that delaminated in the ply itself](/boats/faith.md#a-laminated-hatch-beam-that-delaminated-in-the-ply-itself) and [fastening and gluing — a delamination caution](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#fastening-laminated-ply-panels-a-delamination-caution-from-experience).[46]
- **Copper nails for the forward cabin's ply doublers.** About a dozen copper nails, riveted straight through, fasten the plywood doublers that form the bottom of the forward cabin end and take the front window's lower edge, wherever there is no structure below the deck at that point. See [fastening and gluing — copper nails for the forward cabin's ply doublers](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#copper-nails-for-the-forward-cabins-ply-doublers).[47]
- **A UK build-cost figure, rounded for a newcomer, and no shortcut on time.** Gave a rounded, modest cost estimate and about 18 months of evenings-and-weekends work for a newcomer asking (see the fuller itemized [build-cost accounting](/boats/little-jim.md#weigh-in-and-a-full-build-cost-accounting-januarymarch-2003) already on record for "Little Jim").[48] The build time could not have been significantly cut by taking shortcuts, since a Paradox is not a quick boat to build. See [build effort and workspace — corner-cutting to shorten an 18-month build](/construction/build-effort.md#time).[49]
- **Far Eastern ply and epoxy-quantity advice for a newcomer's shopping list.** Confirmed using the cheaper "Far Eastern" marine ply himself — the 6mm had rather thin outer plies but was otherwise fine "so far" — and answered the recurring how-much-epoxy question with a practical rule of thumb: start with a 25 litre drum and buy more once it runs out, by which point the actual rate of use is clear. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a 2007 UK shopping-list recurrence](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2007-uk-shopping-list-recurrence-far-eastern-ply-and-epoxy-quantity).[50]
- **A microballoon-quantity data point.** A full pound of microballoons ordered for fairing arrived in a bucket labelled 0.3 kg, with some still left over — a concrete real-world figure answering a question two epoxy suppliers had each declined to quantify. See [epoxy pre-coating technique — how much micro-balloon filler a fairing job actually needs](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#how-much-micro-balloon-filler-a-fairing-job-actually-needs).[51]
- **Sawn timber confirmed sufficient, and a ring-nail size substitution.** Confirmed sawn timber (rather than planed) is entirely adequate for cleats and structural members, cleat sizing not being critical to better than about ±5 mm — the same mistake of over-buying planed timber having been made on his own build. Separately confirmed the 2-1/2 in **#8** bronze ring nail as a working substitute where the plans' specified 2-1/2 in #10 is not locally stocked, with the plans' quantities close to accurate in practice. See [boats/johanna.md — sourcing Douglas Fir, and sawn versus planed timber](/boats/johanna.md#sourcing-douglas-fir-and-sawn-versus-planed-timber) and [boats/johanna.md — ring-nail sizing, and a bronze-nail offer](/boats/johanna.md#ring-nail-sizing-and-a-bronze-nail-offer).[52][53]
- **A preference for a thick bottom.** On a stitch-and-glue bottom-thickness debate, finds something "very comforting" about a thick (22 mm) bottom specifically when drying out somewhere unfamiliar and checking for rocks underneath first. See [hull planking — how thick does the bottom really need to be?](/construction/hull-planking.md#how-thick-does-the-bottom-really-need-to-be).[54]
- **A wire-template tiller technique.** Alongside the nail-jig lamination method above, offered a template-free way to find a tiller's needed curve — bend a length of stiff wire (a coat-hanger will do) against the rudder stock, checking clearance as the rudder swings from side to side. See [design/rudder.md — tiller construction](/components/rudder.md#tiller-construction-laminated-wood-versus-bent-metal-tube).[55]
- **A friction tiller-rope lock, and when a lock is actually needed.** Built a simple two-disc clamp — one disc fixed under the side deck, the other on a bolt passed through it — that grips the tiller rope by tightening the bolt disc down. Judged a lock rarely necessary in ordinary sailing, since friction alone usually holds the tiller, but found the tiller line does need to be held or locked when sailing in shallow water with the rudder partly raised, when tiller loads can become surprisingly large. See [self-steering — locking the tiller rope, and why most builders don't bother](/operations/self-steering.md#locking-the-tiller-rope-and-why-most-builders-dont-bother).[56][57]
- **Fixing water-ballast tank tops leak-free, and a chine-log ballast-access method.** Coats the underside of the tank top with thickened glue, then reaches in through the access hatches to work the squeezed-out excess into a fillet by hand — a technique that has kept the tank tops themselves leak-free, unlike the inspection hatches. Separately, secures his own lead ballast by drilling and tapping into the top of each pig and bolting on a plate anchored into the chine log; not particularly rigid, but shows no sign of the pigs shifting, and recommends leaving ballast unfastened until after the first few sails so it can be shifted to trim the boat. When a fellow builder's flap-covered ballast bolts proved hard to reach, suggested cutting small access holes above the bolt heads as an alternative to a removable plank. See [water tank construction and lining — fixing the tank tops in place](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#fixing-the-tank-tops-in-place) and [weight, ballast, and displacement — reaching the ballast fastenings after the floors are in](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#reaching-the-ballast-fastenings-after-the-floors-are-in).[58][59]
- **Why Matt Layden's own foot-drag furling method needs no extra hardware.** A winch-style dead drum, to add drag on the slack line while hoisting or furling, alongside the vent-trunk cleats, is unnecessary: Matt Layden keeps tension on both lines with his foot because he does not fit a topping lift, and any such device would still need a foot (not just a cam cleat) to hold the *other* line's two-handed load. Of his own two departures from the stock plans that he judges genuinely successful — beyond fitting a topping lift — one is a window let into the hatch, and the other is splitting the underfloor locker lids so they can be opened with the seat still down; every other change he tried stemmed from not yet understanding how the stock system worked. See [design/rig.md — why Matt Layden's foot-drag furling method needs no extra hardware](/components/rig/index.md#why-matt-laydens-foot-drag-furling-method-needs-no-extra-hardware).[60]
- **Does not drink his own ballast water.** Found "interesting slime" in the tank after his first season filling from a tap; now fills instead from whatever water he is floating in, using an electric bilge pump, keeping the option to clean the tank out and refill with drinking water for a genuinely long trip. See [water tank construction and lining — fixing the tank tops in place](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#fixing-the-tank-tops-in-place).[61]
- **No fixed pipework to the tanks at all.** The only pipes running to his own ballast tanks are the vents and the deck-mounted fillers shown on the plans; a submersible electric bilge pump dropped into each tank empties it, and a second, roving electric bilge pump fills them — a simpler alternative to a plumbed pump-and-hose system. See [water tank plumbing — a 2016 alternative](/construction/water-tank-plumbing.md#a-2016-alternative-submersible-bilge-pumps-and-a-combined-fill-and-drain-pipe-proposal).[287]
- **Deck-beam cleats left proud, and the sheet-10 corner-post angles explained.** Leaving deck-beam cleats proud and shaping them down once the deck itself goes on for final assembly avoids fighting an already-fixed low cleat; sheet 10's corner-post detail is a section through the cabin's uprights, cross-referenced to the side-frame layout by a linking arrow rather than a true plan view, its two angles setting how the front and rear windows seat once wrapped around the post, the front window needing the larger angle for its greater curve — not critical to get exactly right off the drawing, since it can be tweaked at assembly with an angle grinder. On interior paint, a primer that sticks to epoxy worked well except where applied below its stated minimum temperature in winter, and ordinary exterior house paint should be hard-wearing enough for interior use by the same logic that exterior window frames only need occasional repainting; forward of where the feet sit while sailing, a length of foam sleeping mat carpeting the floor is much easier on the knees than bare plywood, with a gardener's kneeling mat worth having to hand besides. See [deck and cabin beam lamination — corner post angles on sheet 10](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#corner-post-angles-on-sheet-10-the-30-degree-and-14-degree-window-seating-detail), [epoxy pre-coating technique — interior finish](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#interior-finish-bare-wood-epoxy-varnish-cetol-or-paint), and [interior bin panels and hinged floor — cabin-sole non-slip and kneeling comfort](/components/interior.md#cabin-sole-non-slip-and-kneeling-comfort).[62]
- **Why the mast can't simply be moved to the centerline, and the real cost of scaling up for company.** Centering the mast outright, rather than only moving the sail to starboard, would cost the furled sail its ability to self-stow along the side deck and would likely require cutting a hole through the rear baffle to give the sheet a fair run; a 20%-enlarged, roughly 5-metre Paradox carries a considerable displacement increase over stock worth weighing against available car and trailer capacity, and a sailor after safe, comfortable coastal cruising for two — especially with an engine — is generally better served by a different design altogether. See [rig and sail — sail bearing on the mast, and mast offset](/components/rig/index.md#sail-bearing-on-the-mast-and-mast-offset) and [stretched Paradox — carrying company aboard, and the practical cost of going bigger](/design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md#carrying-company-aboard-and-the-practical-cost-of-going-bigger).[63]
- **Distinguishing chine runners from catamaran anti-vortex panels.** Replying to a link on catamaran "anti-vortex panels," judged them a different mechanism from Paradox's own chine runners — the panels are fitted to a cat hull that sails flat, whereas Paradox's leeway reduces specifically as the hull heels. See [how chine runners work — whether a flat bottom is essential to the mechanism](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md#whether-a-flat-bottom-is-essential-to-the-mechanism).[64]
- **A wire-nail tooling-pin technique for hull assembly.** Discovered during Little Jim's own trial assembly that the pilot holes drilled for ring nails were a nice push fit for ordinary wire nails, used as temporary locating pins letting the hull be disassembled and reassembled in exactly the same position and every ring-nail hole pre-drilled before mixing any glue — a technique used at every later stage of the build. See [Little Jim — a wire-nail tooling-pin technique for hull assembly](/boats/little-jim.md#a-wire-nail-tooling-pin-technique-for-hull-assembly) and [hull assembly sequence — gluing up the hull](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#gluing-up-the-hull-one-sitting-or-several-and-locating-the-bulkheads-with-temporary-pins).[65]
- **Cabin-heat: a hatch-chimney clearance caution, and how little heat a cold night actually needs.** A chimney run through the cabin roof must clear the hatch's own swing to avoid it being sliced off when opened; his own coldest night aboard, about -2°C, was comfortable in a sleeping bag alone, and boiling a mug of coffee on waking warmed the cabin enough to dress in before the heat dissipated — the boat's ventilation supplies a single burner stove even with the hatch shut. See [cabin heating — a charcoal or kerosene can heater, and the transom-cook-stove alternative](/operations/cabin-heating.md#a-charcoal-or-kerosene-can-heater-and-the-transom-cook-stove-alternative).[66][67]
- **The design's "why," not its "what."** Answering a builder's list of the design's main elements, argued Paradox is best understood by the specific problems it was built to solve rather than as a checklist of parts — the lug rig, mechanically simple but conceptually inseparable from the rest of the design, would likely produce "a pig's ear" if extracted and fitted to a different hull — and gave his own summary of the design's purpose: carrying the crew in comfort and safety, in shallow water, in relatively poor weather, without demanding anything energetic of them, and just as capably in deep water and good weather. See [paradox.md](/paradox.md#citations).[68]
- **Ring nails versus screws, revisited: removability doesn't matter much in practice.** Judged the screws-are-removable argument for the plans' bronze ring nails largely academic on a real hull, since every joint is already well glued and glassed — pulling a screw causes about as much collateral damage as pulling a barbed nail. If a ring nail really must come out, grind off the head, separate the joint, and cut it off flush; nails remain cheaper, quicker, and more satisfying to drive in the first place. See [fastening and gluing — bronze ring nail cost, and nails versus wood screws](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#bronze-ring-nail-cost-and-nails-versus-wood-screws).[69]
- **Cabin-window condensation: a moulded gutter, and drain holes.** Judging the plans' thin drip-rail strip too vulnerable, moulded a small guttering from thickened epoxy along the cabin-top beam instead, and drilled a 4 mm hole through the lower rear corner of each side window to let any condensation drain away. See [window glazing — cabin condensation](/construction/window-glazing-materials.md#cabin-condensation-a-drip-rail-a-moulded-gutter-and-window-drain-holes).[70]
- **Bow cleats, a stem U-bolt, and a mooring-pickup routine.** The plans' bow cleats, built to plan, take an anchor's strain without trouble; a length of hose pipe sleeved over the rope where it crosses the deck edge stops chafe, and a hole drilled through each cleat between its mounting bolts lets a line run through and back to the stern down each side. Separately described picking up a mooring with a snap hook on the end of the bow line, swapped afterward for a shorter line, and casting off entirely from the cabin using a line doubled from a rear cleat — illustrating that any one anchoring or mooring system is an option, not a fixed rule. See [ground tackle and anchoring — attaching ground tackle at the bow](/operations/ground-tackle.md#attaching-ground-tackle-at-the-bow-cleats-a-stem-u-bolt-and-chafe-protection) and [ground tackle and anchoring — a cabin-fed bucket routine, and treating any system as optional](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-cabin-fed-bucket-routine-and-treating-any-system-as-optional).[71][72]
- **A firsthand heaving-to account: rudder up, sail trimmed, and the effect of reducing sail further.** In a Force 4, lifting the rudder and sheeting the sail in tight left the boat lying head to wind and drifting astern at about a knot; reducing sail further let the bow fall off more, and dropping sail completely left her lying nearly broadside on. See [heavy-weather tactics — a 2008 recurrence: heaving to without a jib](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#a-2008-recurrence-heaving-to-without-a-jib).[73]
- **Gluing the vent trunk's side panel in, rather than leaving it removable.** Considered leaving the starboard vent-trunk side panel removable, and would have sealed it with the same product used on the windows had he done so, but glued it on permanently instead; has never actually felt the need to get inside since, though cutting an access panel later would be straightforward if ever needed. See [vent box — access to the vent box: a removable side panel versus gluing it in place](/components/vent-box.md#access-to-the-vent-box-a-removable-side-panel-versus-gluing-it-in-place).[74]
- **Stepping the mast, and why no stays are needed.** A newcomer's mast-stepping question drew an eight-step answer grounded in "Little Jim"'s own experience: the halyard's own downward tension, once the sail is hoisted, is what holds the mast in its step — it has never shown the least sign of movement, the only known problem on record being the opposite one, a different builder's mast step fit too snugly to remove. See [components/rig/mast.md — stepping the mast](/components/rig/mast.md#stepping-the-mast-a-first-timers-question-answered).[75]
- **A newcomer's cluster of living-aboard questions, answered in full.** Anchors, galley posture, using the heads, and long-passage seat comfort — a first-timer's whole cluster of questions about actually living aboard drew a full firsthand account: three anchors (a 5 kg Bruce, a 10 lb Fisherman, and a folding grapnel) all launched from the cabin onto a permanent bow line; cooking seated, twisting round for simple jobs and kneeling on the floor for more ambitious ones; standing and leaning over the side for the heads; and a rotation of cushions through the day for long-passage seat comfort. See [ground tackle and anchoring](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-third-owners-three-anchor-stowage-and-a-permanent-bow-line-launch-method), [provisioning and water](/operations/provisioning-and-water.md#cooking-in-a-minimal-galley-gimballed-single-burner-stove-no-dedicated-worktop), [head and sanitation arrangements](/operations/head-and-sanitation.md#informal-practice-bucket-and-chuck-it-not-a-fitted-head), and [crew size and cabin fit](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#long-passage-seat-comfort-cushion-practice).[76]

- **A related lower-pintle bond failure, and a suspected cause.** Had his own lower pintle come loose when the surrounding epoxy bond failed, though the hole itself, drilled oversize and lined with epoxy, had kept water out of the wood; re-bonded it after cutting saw kerfs into the pintle and roughening its surface for a better mechanical key, adopting a regular winter check afterward. Suspects the lower pintle takes heavy load, especially under grounding, which can separate poorly-made plywood laminations, for which flush copper rivets might help; also doubted a ready-made transom-hung rudder would survive the same grounding loads unless its stock were too short ever to touch bottom. See [rudder and chine experiments — fitting the lower pintle without drilling the rudder stock](/components/rudder.md#fitting-the-lower-pintle-without-drilling-the-rudder-stock).[77]
- **A point-by-point answer to a newcomer's whole list of feared build steps.** A deck-beam lamination jig need not be dimensionally precise, only good enough to hold curvature, and is manageable single-handed; laying out tight panels is forgiving — draw the shapes, cut outside the line, and trim most parts to size once bonded (see [panel layout and nesting](/construction/panel-layout.md)); the stem, "a fairly large lump," suits an angle grinder and coarse sanding disc, final shape decided at trial assembly (see [notching the stem — shaping the stem timber](/construction/stem-notching.md#shaping-the-stem-timber-an-angle-grinder)); an overshot cut is no disaster, since filled epoxy is stronger than the wood; scarfing side and bottom panels for the first time proved "relatively painless" ganging clamped pieces under an angle grinder rather than a hand plane (see [scarf joints — an angle-grinder method](/construction/scarf-joints.md#an-angle-grinder-method-ganging-multiple-panels-for-one-cut)); mixing epoxy fast enough for a large panel is a matter of batch size and an improvised continuous-mixing stirrer (see [epoxy pre-coating technique — an electric stirrer](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#an-electric-stirrer-for-continuous-large-panel-work)); and none of it need be rushed, since it does the hull no harm to leave it for months at a stretch. See [Paradox as a first boat — an itemized list of first-timer worries](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#an-itemized-list-of-first-timer-worries-and-why-mistakes-are-recoverable).[78]
- **A personal answer on marine ply choice and aging.** Asked directly what grade he had used and how it aged, gave his own build's specifics: the cheapest BS1088-certified marine ply available locally, never inspected again once fully skinned in epoxy/glass and paint outside and epoxy/polystyrene inside; would reach for ACX from a home center only where the plans' exterior grade could not be sourced at all, the design being over-engineered enough to absorb a little void-related weakness regardless. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a 2009 recurrence](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2009-recurrence-doubling-thinner-sheets-instead-of-a-single-thick-panel-and-marine-versus-ac-grade-from-a-working-shipwrights-experience).[79]
- **Bulkhead 3's nailed edge joint, confirmed from a real build.** Nails at bulkhead 3 go in as two locating pins per side into pre-drilled holes in the bulkhead's own edge, not into a cleat — the plans' one deliberate exception to nailing only into cleats. See [hull assembly sequence — bulkhead #3 has no side cleats, by design](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#bulkhead-3-has-no-side-cleats-by-design).[80]
- **Workspace sequencing, and why nails beat stitch-and-glue on Paradox specifically.** Build the small parts — mast, spars, rudder — before the hull goes together, while the workspace is still clear, so the boat is ready to launch the day the hull itself is finished rather than leaving loose ends; and prefer the plans' nailed method over stitch-and-glue for speed, since any weight stitch-and-glue would save just has to be bought back as lead ballast anyway. See [build effort and workspace](/construction/build-effort.md#workspace) and [fastening and gluing — why nails, not stitch-and-glue](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#why-nails-not-stitch-and-glue-a-weight-trade-off-paradox-doesnt-need).[81]
- **Deck panels nailed like everything else, and left unfaired on purpose.** Nailed his own deck panels down the same way as the rest of the hull, hammering the heads flush before glassing over them with light cloth and painting, rather than fairing them in, so their positions stay identifiable and can be avoided later when screwing anything else to the deck. See [deck and cabin beam lamination — fastening the deck panels](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#fastening-the-deck-panels-nailing-versus-screwing-and-a-temporary-screw-clamp-technique).[233]
- **A further sanitation verdict: port-a-potties, and the case for a Wag Bag.** Has seen a port-a-pottie carried in another Paradox's forward stateroom, needing to be pulled to the hatch each time it was used — not very convenient; failing "bucket and chuck it," recommends a commercial Wag Bag kit. See [head and sanitation arrangements — a further 2010 recurrence](/operations/head-and-sanitation.md#a-further-2010-recurrence-the-great-lakes-regulatory-case-an-unsuitable-factory-head-and-a-wag-bag-recommendation).[241]
- **A mushroom-valve tiller-port baffle for the rudder hole.** A loose rubber disc fitted on the tiller aft of the transom passes air freely in normal use but presses shut against the transom when a wave slaps the stern, needing only to slow the water rather than stop it outright. See [vent baffle — two 2011 DIY tiller-port baffle designs](/components/vent-baffle.md#two-2011-diy-tiller-port-baffle-designs).[243][244]
- **A forepeak tub, a bungee-fence bin, and a floor dropped for a stowed bicycle.** Keeps a watertight tub of emergency clothing, a towel, and a space blanket in the forepeak all season; lines the cabin floor with garage-style rubber matting for comfort while sliding in; replaced the forward storage area's internal partition with a bungee-cord fence and dropped its floor to stow a bicycle, which also frees room to put feet through for extra sleeping length. See [interior — forepeak stowage and retrieval, a 2017 exchange](/components/interior.md#forepeak-stowage-and-retrieval-a-2017-exchange).[288]

## Rig, electrics, and furling

- **A further mast-wiring recurrence, and a hindsight verdict on the tricolour.** Answering a newcomer's masthead-wiring question in November 2010, described embedding a waterproof socket into the mast just above its solid base, the mating plug reached and plugged in from inside the cabin through the vent box, and warned that a bare wire-and-socket left exposed at that point on some other builds can catch and become "a pain" whenever the mast is stepped or unstepped for trailering. Asked in turn how his own compact masthead light (below) was built, judged with several years' hindsight that the tricolour sectors overlap too much in practice and that, built again, only the white all-around light — legal under COLREGs for a boat this size — would be worth fitting. See [electrical wiring — a further November 2010 recurrence](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#a-further-november-2010-recurrence-and-a-caution-against-an-exposed-mast-base-connector) and [electrical wiring — a 2010 hindsight verdict](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#a-2010-hindsight-verdict-sector-overlap-and-a-case-for-skipping-the-tricolour-on-a-boat-this-size).[239][240]

- **Steering-line forward-block mounting.** Screwed the forward corner blocks of the internal steering-line loop directly to the intermediate deck beam, a simpler fix than the plans' own drawn position, suspended in mid air tight against bulkhead 2. See [Internal steering — where the corner blocks go](/components/steering.md#where-the-corner-blocks-go-the-plans-against-practice).[82]
- **A side-mounted electric outboard on "Little Jim."** Fitted an electric motor to the hull's side, rather than the transom, specifically to avoid keeping petrol inside the cabin, using brass hinges with a removable pin braced by a forward strut after an earlier, less sturdy bracket distorted at full throttle; the motor cannot stay mounted while sailing and needs only slight angling to hold a straight course under power. See [auxiliary outboard power — further field experience with a side-mounted outboard](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#further-field-experience-with-a-side-mounted-outboard).[83]
- **Publishing the outboard-bracket photos, and reacting to a fellow builder's side mount.** A side-mounted bracket built for another Paradox, "Ironsides," was called an elegant solution, while cautioning it might ship water while being fitted afloat; separately published his own removable, side-mounted outboard-bracket photos on his website (with a change-of-address notice after the previous site was overwhelmed by spam), noting the mount keeps the motor clear of the sheet for motor-sailing so long as the heel angle stays short of submerging the outboard's head. See [auxiliary outboard power — a worked side-mount installation, and the risk of shipping water while fitting it afloat](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#a-worked-side-mount-installation-and-the-risk-of-shipping-water-while-fitting-it-afloat) and [an outboard bracket published for reference](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#an-outboard-bracket-published-for-reference).[84][85]
- **How the mast steps and unsteps.** The mast foot simply sits on a hull-bottom block, held upright by the deck partners, so lowering it is only a matter of lifting it about 600 mm clear before laying it down on the rolled sail and boom — distinct from Little Cruiser's pinned, hinged-strut folding mast. See [mast partners and vent trunk — no mast-lowering pin on Paradox, unlike Little Cruiser](/components/rig/mast.md#no-mast-lowering-pin-on-paradox-unlike-little-cruiser).
- **Resolving the mast drawing's recurring confusion (2016).** Gave the fullest published reading yet of drawing sheet 12's three-view, mixed-scale layout — separating the stray drafting-error arrows from the real dimensions, and working out exactly which board each of the sheet's figures describes — for a builder rebuilding his own mast for weight. See [mast — reading the mast drawing](/components/rig/mast.md#reading-the-mast-drawing-a-recurring-point-of-confusion).[285]
- **A vane-driven independent auxiliary rudder, and a bow-mounted vane idea.** Wondered whether a wind vane could drive a small, entirely independent rudder — applying only correcting adjustments while the main rudder holds the general course — mounted right forward on the foredeck, where there is ample room clear of the sails; did not expect to fit one to "Little Jim" himself. See [self-steering — an independent, vane-driven auxiliary rudder, and a bow-mounted alternative](/operations/self-steering.md#an-independent-vane-driven-auxiliary-rudder-and-a-bow-mounted-alternative).[86]
- **A single salvaged NiCad battery covers a full season's power needs.** A 12V wet NiCad battery from 1977, replaced under routine coal-industry maintenance, charged once each spring runs a GPS, echo sounder, running lights, and water-ballast/bilge pump all season with no further attention — a real-world power-budget counterpoint to how much generating capacity a Paradox might actually need (raised against a scale mockup of a wind generator and radar). See [radio and electronics — a wind generator and radar: a scale-mockup exercise, and how little power a Paradox actually needs](/operations/radio-and-electronics.md#a-wind-generator-and-radar-a-scale-mockup-exercise-and-how-little-power-a-paradox-actually-needs).
- **A compact LED masthead light.** A single masthead unit under **2 in diameter and 1.5 in tall** can combine a tricolour and an all-around white light, built with LEDs (roughly a tenth the draw of a filament bulb) and fed by just **two wires** wired in opposite polarity to switch functions; a steaming light is unnecessary without an engine. Wiring left loose and extractable inside the mast can be replaced without cutting the mast open. See [running lights](/operations/running-lights.md). A two-part LED write-up covers the underlying current/voltage theory and the series-wiring technique for maximizing light output per watt, followed by a full construction method (LED counts per sector, five-circuit reverse-polarity wiring, silicone-potted circuit boards) for a sector'd tricolour/anchor light — see [running lights — LED electrical theory, and how to wire several in series](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#led-electrical-theory-and-how-to-wire-several-in-series) and [building a sector'd LED masthead light](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#building-a-sectord-led-masthead-light-led-counts-wiring-and-potting). Answering a fellow UK builder's below-deck wiring question in October 2005, described his own bottom-of-mast arrangement in full: a water-resistant plug/socket mounted low on the mast's aft face, reached from inside the boat by "a bit of a contortionist trick," with spare wire left coiled inside the mast so the connection (or the whole run, if damaged) can be remade without stripping it out. See [running lights — clearing a snug deck hole as the mast is stepped and unstepped](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#clearing-a-snug-deck-hole-as-the-mast-is-stepped-and-unstepped). **How much the nav lights actually get used, in practice.** In the five years since launching, the tricolour has been switched on only once — with, so far as could be told, no one nearby to see it — and the all-round white only occasionally, at anchor; a light hoisted temporarily in the topping lift would have served the anchor-light role just as well. In practice, a powerful hand-held torch inspires more confidence than either fitted light. See [running lights — how often navigation lights actually get used, and a hand-torch alternative](/operations/running-lights.md#how-often-navigation-lights-actually-get-used-and-a-hand-torch-alternative).[87]
- **Furling and the topping lift.** Stock furling gear is impractical without a topping lift; a **continuous halyard/furling-line** arrangement (a single line, so hauling the halyard pays out the furling line in step) trades away the ability to drop all sail instantly for a cleaner cabin — see [furling system](/components/rig/furling.md). Sailing without a topping lift is possible but much simpler with one; see [furling system — a UK builder's topping-lift question](/components/rig/furling.md#a-uk-builders-topping-lift-question). Asked again over a year later, restated the same conclusion from direct experience — without one, the boom drops onto the cabin roof and bangs about as the halyard is slackened — and noted his own fairly heavy (about 6 oz) sail cloth likely worsens the problem. See [furling system — a second, later topping-lift question, and two more confirmations](/components/rig/furling.md#a-second-later-topping-lift-question-and-two-more-confirmations).[88] Normally slackens the topping lift again once sail is set, but will leave it tight instead when a reef is expected soon — as when working up a winding channel with the wind broadly astern, shortening sail progressively corner by corner while the lift stays set throughout. See [furling system — anticipating an upcoming reef](/components/rig/furling.md#anticipating-an-upcoming-reef-leaving-the-topping-lift-set).[89] Confirmed the stock plans' furl direction from Sheet 9 — the sail and furling line come to the port (outside) side of the boom, which has worked without trouble on his own boat — wondering only whether reversing it would introduce mast chafe from the sail rubbing the other way. See [furling system — a tacking-direction asymmetry with the furl direction](/components/rig/furling.md#a-tacking-direction-asymmetry-with-the-furl-direction).[90]
- **The reefing triangle and boom taper.** Confirmed the foam-in-the-boom-end fix a second time, and offered a simpler workaround: stuffing the extra sail material behind the storage hoop once furled. See [rig and sail — the reefing triangle, and why reshaping the boom taper is the wrong fix](/components/rig/index.md#the-reefing-triangle-and-why-reshaping-the-boom-taper-is-the-wrong-fix). Later explained the underlying mechanics from three seasons' use: where the sail sits on the boom while furling is set by the boom's taper and the sail's tack angle, not by any fence around the drum (a fence would only jam), the taller Mk2 sail wanting to move forward toward the drum as it rolls up; he corrected his own phrasing to "taper of *boom*" and noted he had retrofit his own under-tapered boom with a rigid-foam wedge at the clew end.[91] See [furling system — the Mk1/Mk2 tack-angle difference, and why the sail can't be fenced onto the drum](/components/rig/furling.md#the-mk1mk2-tack-angle-difference-and-why-the-sail-cant-be-fenced-onto-the-drum). Gave his own Mk1 boom's worked taper dimensions (50 x 38 mm at the clew, 35 x 38 mm at the tack, about 30 mm difference in circumference), estimated the Mk2 sail's forward creep at about 2.5 in (citing a fellow builder's figure), and reported furling is as easy as it looks in the WaterTribe video, apart from rope blisters until the hands harden. See [furling system — worked boom dimensions for the Mk1 taper](/components/rig/furling.md#worked-boom-dimensions-for-the-mk1-taper).[92][93]
- **Masthead sheave choice.** A Tufnol sheave on a stainless pin at the masthead beats a plain grooved "dumb sheave": since hauling up the sail is the single most physically demanding task aboard, a proper bearing there is worthwhile. See [rig and sail — a dumb sheave versus a bearing sheave at the masthead](/components/rig/index.md#a-dumb-sheave-versus-a-bearing-sheave-at-the-masthead).
- **Halyard fixing point on the yard.** Ties the halyard round the top spar through a hoop screwed to its underside near the sail's own gap in the cloth, movable if needed and left loose until the sail can be hoisted and checked. See [rig and sail — halyard fixing point on the spar: an open question](/components/rig/index.md#halyard-fixing-point-on-the-spar-an-open-question).
- **Why the yard is tapered.** As a professional engineer, explained that a spar needs to be stiffest where it is restrained — for the yard, where it bears against the mast — with the taper concentrating stiffness there while shedding weight elsewhere; still recommended trying a constant-diameter bamboo pole as a cheap, easily-replaced experiment. See [rig and sail — why yard and boom shape matter for pointing](/components/rig/index.md#why-yard-and-boom-shape-matter-for-pointing).
