- **The yard's own material: Douglas fir, still going strong.** Confirmed his own DF yard has needed no attention in thirteen years' use, answering a fellow builder weighing DF against a denser wood or an aluminum tube. See [rig and sail — yard material: Douglas fir remains standard, aluminum a proven alternative](/components/rig/index.md#yard-material-douglas-fir-remains-standard-aluminum-a-proven-alternative).[268]
- **The yard as a double cantilever, and a mast-chafe fix.** Restated the taper's engineering logic in stronger terms — the yard is a double cantilever, so the bending moment (and the stiffness needed to resist it) falls away with distance from the mast — and separately fixed his own mast-chafe problem by lengthening his sail's sleeve gap so the spar, not the cloth, rubs the mast, combined with a leather sleeve on the mast itself; also floated a mast-mounted yard traveller, borrowed from a Welsford Houdini dinghy, to keep the yard close to the mast at all points of reefing. See [rig and sail — mast chafe from the yard, and finishes that prevent it](/components/rig/index.md#mast-chafe-from-the-yard-and-finishes-that-prevent-it) and [rig and sail — a proposed mast-mounted traveller to keep the yard close when reefed](/components/rig/index.md#a-proposed-mast-mounted-traveller-to-keep-the-yard-close-when-reefed).[94]
- **Yuloh pivot construction, blade width, and reading Drawing #12.** A yuloh can be laminated from two planks bent over a 30 mm-high block (free ends elevated over the block and loaded with weights until they touch the ground), with the pivot bolt built from 10 mm stainless studding with a spherical-bearing ball epoxied on the end, seated in a recessed tufnol-block socket drilled to the ball's diameter. A loop of bungee cord — a system credited to [David Bolduc](/people/bolduc/dave.md) — fixes the yuloh's tendency to jump off the ball, trading a few seconds' setup time for a more secure fit than Matt Layden's own unfixed arrangement. Confirmed the plans' blade width as 3.5 in (90 mm) at its widest point, restated the 30 mm-block lamination method (described the other way round from his own 2004 account, left unreconciled), and restated the yuloh pivot's handle-ward move at about 4 in rather than the 150 mm figure already on record — see [yuloh — building the yuloh itself](/components/yuloh.md#building-the-yuloh-itself-blade-curvature-and-the-pivot-boltsocket) for both discrepancies, neither resolved. Also answered a first-time builder's confusion reading Drawing #12's squared spar sections, giving the yard's and boom's cross-sections at specific distances along their length and confirming the yuloh's flatter side sits uppermost in use — see [references/building-plans.md — reading the plans](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions).[95][96] Answering the same builder's follow-up hand-tools-only question, described his own shaping method — a hand plane or coarse sanding disc in an angle grinder for bulk removal, then a strop of sandpaper worked over each rounded corner for a smooth curve, checked against cardboard templates cut to each section.[97] Weighing whether the blade needs to be curved end to end or only near the blade, judged it probably doesn't matter much either way, though the full-length curve does stow more neatly on deck.[98] Separately clarified that the blade's own 30 mm curve merely shifts the handle forward and down by about the same amount, a modest change on its own, and that moving the pivot hole itself (see above) was partly to stop the handle striking his face on the pull stroke.[99] Restated the same lift mechanism once more for a builder confused by the plans, likening the blade to an aircraft wing or a propeller blade producing lift by its shape and angle of attack — "just like those awkward things, a keel or centre-board, that some sailors still insist on putting up with" — and, on a secondhand report elsewhere of a straight vertical oar out-pacing a yuloh, doubted how a vertical blade would even work as propulsion, restating that speed is not the only thing that matters: energy efficiency counts too, and driving his own boat at a sustainable, unhurried 2 knots leaves a hand free besides.[100][101][102] See [yuloh — building the yuloh itself](/components/yuloh.md#building-the-yuloh-itself-blade-curvature-and-the-pivot-boltsocket) and [yuloh — degree of longitudinal curvature](/components/yuloh.md#blade-camber-the-ford-nomenclature-and-pivot-socket-dimensions) for the fuller discussion.
- **A mainsheet-load testing method, and a question about Matt's single-line rig.** Absent any calculated figure for the loads a mainsheet arch or fitting must withstand, an empirical test works instead — hook a spring balance onto the sheet while sailing and pull it out to the angle the rail would hold it — noting the load likely varies considerably by point of sail. Matt Layden's own single-line mainsheet was his everyday rig rather than a race modification; a photograph in the Sailing Magazine coverage of the design shows his actual sheeting method, raising an unresolved question of why the plans instead draw a two-part sheet. See [rig and sail — mainsheet fouling on the rudder and rear deck with an outboard fitted](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#mainsheet-fouling-on-the-rudder-and-rear-deck-with-an-outboard-fitted).
- **Mainsheet-and-outboard fouling, and stability at the stern.** Paradox's longer boom (versus Little Cruiser's shorter one) forces the mainsheet to a further-aft angle, ruling out a deck-mounted sheet since it would foul the boom-rolling swivel and the motor housing alike; a metal hoop cage around a mounted outboard is not worth building, an electric outboard stowed inside being the better alternative. Stability itself is not a practical problem for a Paradox: three adults have stood on the aft deck at once, underway, without incident, though the boat was not tied on and the sail not fully hoisted at the time. See [rig and sail — a horse fitted to clear the outboard, and the mainsheet-angle physics behind it](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#a-horse-fitted-to-clear-the-outboard-and-the-mainsheet-angle-physics-behind-it) and [self-righting and capsize recovery — stability under crew load](/design/self-righting-and-capsize-recovery.md#stability-under-crew-load-standing-at-the-stern).
- **The transom-radius dimension.** The plans' missing transom-radius figure is **57 mm**, worked back from the deck-beam info on plan sheet 10 and confirmed problem-free at deck-fitting — independently cross-checked against a digital model of the plans; see [panel layout and nesting — the missing transom-radius dimension, resolved](/construction/panel-layout.md#the-missing-transom-radius-dimension-resolved).
- **Sail and rig data.** His own UK-made sail was tan and **6 oz** cloth — the archive's heaviest recorded cloth weight; the sail bears against the mast on the starboard tack, and the mast is offset to port — see [rig and sail](/components/rig/index.md). The original Paradox carried a single-part sheet, changed on the plans to a two-part purchase for the mechanical advantage of hauling it in, with the fixed end at a port-side transom eye offset to match the off-center mast; see [rig and sail — sheet entry through the transom: prototype versus plans](/components/rig/index.md#sheet-entry-through-the-transom-prototype-versus-plans). The mast's rearward rake and the center-of-gravity mark ("C/A") are given on plan page 1 — see [rig and sail](/components/rig/index.md) and [weight, ballast, and displacement](/design/weight-and-ballast.md).
- **Ballast figures, and a caution against bonding lead in permanently.** The plans' ballast range exists to let lead be removed as stores are added, not as a build-weight margin; ballast trim can take several outings after launch to get right — a caution against any scheme (sheet lead laminated into the bottom, or bonded on as an outer skin) that can't be adjusted afterward. A representative real figure: 120 kg of lead (96 kg forward of bulkhead #3, 24 kg behind bulkhead #4) bringing the boat to 640 kg overall, rechecked each spring as gear accumulates. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — a laminated sheet-lead bottom as an alternative to loose pigs](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#a-laminated-sheet-lead-bottom-as-an-alternative-to-loose-pigs-speculative).
- **Reading the mast's plan views, a second time.** The plans' three confusing mast profile views and the stave-assembly sequence resolve with a mold-and-laminate reading, plus a practical tip of building the mast step and vent trunk onto bulkhead 2 before hull assembly — see [mast — reading the mast drawing](/components/rig/mast.md#reading-the-mast-drawing-a-recurring-point-of-confusion). A simple field fix — a bit of foam taped to the boom's outboard end to take up a taller sail's extra unfurled cloth — is worth trying with any taller sail; see [rig and sail — quantifying the new sailplan's effect on boom furling geometry](/components/rig/index.md#quantifying-the-new-sailplans-effect-on-boom-furling-geometry).
- **Two further deviations from stock.** Considered mounting the anchor on the rear deck rather than the bow, and omitted the mast's lightning protection, reasoning that kind of storm is not much of a problem in the UK.[103]
- **A full, self-written accounting of every departure from the stock plans.** Prompted by a beach conversation with a passerby who assumed "Little Jim" was built exactly to plan, wrote out every change made under three headings — construction-time changes, changes made from experience, and untried ideas for a future build — the fullest single-boat modification accounting on record. See [Little Jim — a full accounting of departures from the stock plans](/boats/little-jim.md#a-full-accounting-of-departures-from-the-stock-plans) for the full list, including a corrected floor-hatch detail (four covers, not two, as this page had previously recorded only vaguely) and a rudder up-haul shackle fix.[103]
- **Fitting a depth sounder on the transom.** A depth sounder's transducer cannot transmit through the wood and epoxy if mounted inside the hull; through-hulling also leaves a through-hull transducer vulnerable when drying out or trailering unless flush-mounted, so a transom mount, tested first on a temporary bracket, is the more practical choice. See [GPS and navigation instruments — fitting a depth sounder or fishfinder](/operations/gps-navigation.md#fitting-a-depth-sounder-or-fishfinder-without-through-hulling-the-bottom).
- **The case for a sounder in soft-mud, mixed-tide sailing grounds.** In narrow channels flanked by shelving mud banks, the choice without a sounder is short tacking or risking a grounding that isn't obvious until speed is already lost. A petrol outboard is too dangerous on such waters, since anything left on deck on a lengthy passage risks being washed off. See [GPS and navigation instruments — fitting a depth sounder or fishfinder](/operations/gps-navigation.md#fitting-a-depth-sounder-or-fishfinder-without-through-hulling-the-bottom).
- **Repeatedly stuck in irons in the Salcombe estuary, and a small-jib idea.** Sailing "Little Jim" one weekend in the Salcombe estuary produced repeated head-to-wind irons that could only be recovered with the yuloh — cured on one occasion instead by gybing round — suggesting a small jib would help push the bow through in that situation. See [rig and sail — a speculative small jib](/components/rig/index.md#a-speculative-small-jib) and [sailing characteristics — a firsthand tacking/grounding lesson](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#a-firsthand-tackinggrounding-lesson).
- **A tack-strut compression failure, corrected.** Two seasons of a lightweight copper-pipe tack tie-down strut crumpling revealed that the strut carries real compression load whenever the sail is reefed, not just tension while furled — the boom's weight plus the reefing line's added tension proved too much for the original part, fixed with a stronger water-pipe-and-stainless-vee-end strut. A second builder independently hit the same failure and settled it for good with a bronze (brass) Schedule 40 pipe strut. See [tack strop — the tack strut also carries compression, not just tension](/components/rig/tack-strop.md#the-tack-strut-also-carries-compression-not-just-tension).
- **Wrapping a Mark II sail on the boom.** Answered a fellow builder's sail-wrap question with his own topping-lift fix for the luff climbing over the drum, confirmed the tack need not align exactly with the mast since the sail's position along the boom sets the rig's balance, and advised tapering the boom thinnest at the tack end — his own under-tapered boom needed packing with rigid foam instead. See [rig and sail — wrapping a Mark II sail on the boom: tack position, balance, and taper](/components/rig/index.md#wrapping-a-mark-ii-sail-on-the-boom-tack-position-balance-and-taper).
- **Boom orientation and yard sag.** On "Little Jim" the boom has no fixed top or bottom, since tightening the reefing line after hoisting simply settles it with whichever side ends up uppermost; on a fellow builder's persisting yard-sag question, recommended working on the yard's own curvature rather than having the sail recut, since a yard can be swapped back to standard if an experiment fails. See [rig and sail — which side of the boom and yard faces up, and curing persistent yard sag](/components/rig/index.md#which-side-of-the-boom-and-yard-faces-up-and-curing-persistent-yard-sag).
- **Rudder-blade shaping, wear protection, and a files-section mix-up.** Clarified that photographs in the group's files section showing a rudder being fitted with lead ballast are of Little Cruiser's rudder, not Paradox's own. Advised photocopying the plan's aerofoil section to full size for cardboard templates, favoring a square trailing edge over a sharp one for less drag and more robustness, keeping the stock-bearing face flat and blended into the foil shape, and leaving the uphaul-groove radius at full thickness. Reported his own leading-edge/bottom wear fix — a split length of copper water pipe hammered round the leading edge and bottom and bonded with epoxy — sound after three seasons aside from the paint wearing off, alongside a Duckworks-sourced alternative of a dacron-rope-filled groove. See [components/rudder.md — rudder ballast without a lead casting](/components/rudder.md#rudder-ballast-without-a-lead-casting) and [components/rudder.md — a proven copper-pipe leading edge](/components/rudder.md#a-proven-copper-pipe-leading-edge-and-a-dacron-rope-alternative).[104]
- **What a dado is.** Explained an unfamiliar plans term to a newcomer as a recess the end of a divider fits into, akin to the mortice in a mortice-and-tenon joint. See [construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md — bulkhead construction details](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#bulkhead-construction-details-not-spelled-out-on-the-plans).[105]
- **Confirming the tiller-freedom effect on "Ironsides," and a fender fix.** Responding to "Ironsides"' launch, confirmed the hands-off-tiller finding as a familiar pattern rather than a surprise — people routinely disbelieve it until they've experienced it themselves, the same effect already established for his own boat; described his own fender fix, eyes fitted to the side of the cabin top, workable but requiring the port fenders' removal before hoisting sail, later adopted for "Ironsides" too. See [boats/ironsides.md — the launch](/boats/ironsides.md#al-laws-reply-the-tiller-freedom-confirmed-a-fender-rigging-fix-and-an-open-question-about-sleeping-two), [boats/ironsides.md — answering Al Law](/boats/ironsides.md#answering-al-law-a-partners-first-impression-and-the-sleeping-two-question-left-for-another-day), and [self-steering — light-air self-steering via tiller-rope friction](/operations/self-steering.md#light-air-self-steering-via-tiller-rope-friction).[106][107]
- **His own masthead topping-lift arrangement, in detail.** Answering a fellow builder's third topping-lift question, described his own eye (not a block) screwed to the aft side of the mast, carrying a two-part span to port of the mast but starboard of the sail — never fouled in use, the only care needed being to keep the yard on the correct side of the ropes when hauling, and the aft span doubles as a place to tie off the halyard and topping lift so they don't slap the mast at night. See [furling system — a third topping-lift question: reading the deck-tube layout at the mast](/components/rig/furling.md#a-third-topping-lift-question-reading-the-deck-tube-layout-at-the-mast).[108]
- **Tack-pipe deck wear, resolved.** Answering a newcomer's worry that the tack strop's brass tube might chew a divot into the deck, explained that a flared pipe end does far less damage than a cut end, that a small glass/epoxy pad round the deck fitting helps further, but the paint there will scratch regardless — a bonded bronze or stainless disc solves it outright for a builder who wants the problem gone entirely. See [rig and sail — tack-pipe deck wear](/components/rig/index.md#tack-pipe-deck-wear).[109]
- **Why the mainsheet at the helmsman's back is not awkward.** Explained that the sheet is pulled in by thrusting forward with the arm while bracing the back against bulkhead 4, with the slack falling into a bin behind the bulkhead where it cannot tangle round the feet — unlike a dinghy, the sheet is set once and left alone rather than played continuously. See [rig and sail — why the mainsheet presented at the helmsman's back is not awkward in practice](/components/rig/index.md#why-the-mainsheet-presented-at-the-helmsmans-back-is-not-awkward-in-practice).[110]
- **Deck loops in copper pipe, and ashore spar storage.** His own deck retaining loops are copper water pipe rather than brass tube, glued into a block under the deck; being tube they are the boat's weakest exterior fitting, guarded against by keeping the starboard loop as low a semicircle as possible so lines can't wrap round it. He stores the mast, boom, and sail in his home garage between outings rather than leaving them aboard, removing the furling axle first, since loading the boat for a trip would be difficult with the spars still inside. See [Deck — deck-loop material choices, and a fastening variant](/components/deck.md#deck-loop-material-choices-and-a-fastening-variant) and [Deck — storing the mast, boom, and sail when not sailing](/components/deck.md#storing-the-mast-boom-and-sail-when-not-sailing).[111][112]
- **Laminating a tiller around a nail jig.** Banged a row of nails into a board tracing the desired curve, then laminated the tiller's wood strips directly around them — a simpler alternative needing no separate forming jig. See [design/rudder.md — tiller construction: laminated wood versus bent metal tube](/components/rudder.md#tiller-construction-laminated-wood-versus-bent-metal-tube).[113]
- **Confirming the pintles mount on the centerline.** Answered a question on whether the rudderstock, gudgeons, and pintles should hang on the transom's centerline: his own are, which puts the rudder blade itself very slightly to starboard and the tiller very slightly to port — a minor, purely cosmetic asymmetry. See [design/rudder.md — rudder pivot point on the transom centerline](/components/rudder.md#rudder-pivot-point-on-the-transom-centerline).[114]
- **The rear-deck-entry question resolved, and a claw fitting for the boom-end sheet.** A deck-entry sheet like Little Cruiser's was considered and set aside, since the downward pull's effect on the sail's set was too uncertain to weigh without a kicking strap fitted; the real fix for a fouled motor is bringing the boom's end further inboard or freeing the sheet from its tip altogether, for which a claw fitting riding loose on the boom, held in place by a longitudinal bridle wire, is one option — moot on his own boat once he found he had no need for a motor at all. Recounted an unplanned gybe wrapping the loose sheet around a cleat or the yuloh pin, easily freed by hand but far harder to clear from a mounted motor. See [rig and sail — the rear-deck-entry question resolved: attachment point, not entry point, is the real hazard](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#the-rear-deck-entry-question-resolved-attachment-point-not-entry-point-is-the-real-hazard).[115][116]
- **A pushpit for the sheet, and self-steering vane-gear clearance.** Suggested fitting a pushpit to lift and guide the sheet clear of a mounted motor as a further fouling remedy; on whether a self-steering vane gear would also be at risk, judged the part most likely to snag sits aft of the boom's own end, out of the sheet's reach. See [rig and sail — a pushpit as a further fouling remedy, and self-steering vane-gear clearance confirmed](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#a-pushpit-as-a-further-fouling-remedy-and-self-steering-vane-gear-clearance-confirmed).[117]
- **A waterproof mast-foot connector, and a caution on side-below-deck exits.** Fits a waterproof connector at the mast's foot, feeding the lead in from inside through the vent; cautions that bringing a wire out through the mast's side below deck level (as another builder does) makes fitting and removing the mast harder, recommending an above-deck exit for any side route instead. See [electrical wiring — a 2008 recurrence: resolved above deck, through the vent box](/construction/electrical-wiring.md#a-2008-recurrence-resolved-above-deck-through-the-vent-box).[118]
- **Predicting turbulence at an un-filleted chine-runner corner.** Agreed a fellow builder built his chine runners to plan, and expected it would be easy enough to retrofit a temporary fillet for comparison if the un-filleted corner underperformed; his own prediction was that omitting the fillet would generate a lot of turbulence right in the corner. See [chine runner construction — the inboard fillet](/chine-runners/construction.md#the-inboard-fillet-an-aerodynamic-case-for-omitting-it-versus-a-turbulence-caution).[119]
- **A tapped-epoxy boom-tang fix, and a flexing rig noticed on a shared cruise.** Fixed his own boom-tang bolt hole by drilling it oversized, filling with epoxy and fibres, then re-drilling and tapping directly for the 8 mm bolt used throughout, with no trouble since; also endorsed a fellow builder's rounded-boom-end fix, having fitted his own 10 mm spacer to the same effect; sailing hard together on the recent Scilly Isles cruise left him newly struck by how much the boom and yard actually flex under load. See [mainsheet, outboard, and rudder fouling — boom-end splitting at the mainsheet-tang bolt](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#boom-end-splitting-at-the-mainsheet-tang-bolt-and-a-construction-fix) and [Little Jim — a postscript on the cruise](/boats/little-jim.md#the-2008-scilly-isles-cruise-little-jims-side-of-the-log).[120]
- **A motor's psychological value on a scheduled, companioned crossing.** Without the outboard on the Scilly crossing there would have been considerably more waiting for the right wind — "if you have a timetable you really do need a motor" — a reluctance he judged psychological rather than physical, having made longer coastal passages without a motor "without a second thought" when sailing alone and unhurried; the large stock of stainless 8 mm bolts already on hand was the only reason that size was used throughout the boom-tang fix above, rather than any particular preference. See [sculling and yuloh technique — Matt Layden's "motors are a luxury" philosophy, put into practice](/operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md#matt-laydens-motors-are-a-luxury-philosophy-put-into-practice).[121]
- **Weighing a side-mounted motor-and-fuel sponson against the transom, for stability reasons.** Answering a fellow builder's sketch for a side-mounted motor bracket stretched to also hold a fuel can, judged the location workable for fouling clearance (as on his own electric outboard) but unsafe for real sailing unless waterproofed — the lee rail runs close to submerged on a fast reach, and even beating to windward in a moderate Force 3 the boat heels around 20 degrees by her own stability curve; recommended keeping any carried fuel on the aft deck against the cabin window instead, clear of gear and waves. See [auxiliary outboard power — a side-mounted motor-and-fuel sponson considered and rejected](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#a-side-mounted-motor-and-fuel-sponson-considered-and-rejected-a-three-position-transom-bracket).[122]
- **Pointing a newcomer to a search engine, and the furling axle's long-term bearing fix.** Answered a newcomer's "where is the URL" question about a Sailrite listing with a search suggestion rather than a link. Separately, answering a newbie's ocean-passage spares questions on the "Force 8 gale" thread, confirmed his own tack-arrangement spacer had once bent from an underbuilt first attempt, explained the boom's own furling axle is held forward mainly by gravity rather than by its retaining screw, and set out the bearing fix that has kept it running trouble-free for years — doming the axle's end, brazing a cap onto a length of water pipe bonded into the boom, and running the axle as a greased, rattling-loose fit inside it — judging a sealed bearing a retrograde step by comparison. See [furling system — a bearing fix for the furling axle's forward thrust](/components/rig/furling.md#a-bearing-fix-for-the-furling-axles-forward-thrust) and [heavy-weather tactics — clarifying the offshore spares list](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#clarifying-the-offshore-spares-list-the-tack-arrangement-spacer-and-the-furling-axles-field-record).[123][124]
- **Restating the tack-spacer and boom-tang fixes for a new correspondent.** A year on from the tack-strut rebuild and the boom-tang epoxy fix, restated both unchanged when a different correspondent asked: the spacer is water pipe capped with a stainless steel U-piece, the plans' own Schedule 40 brass pipe still being hard to find in the UK; the tang bolt hole is drilled, filled with epoxy, then drilled and tapped for an 8mm bolt, made "good and beefy" against the mainsheet's forward-pulling bending load. See [tack strop — the tack strut also carries compression, not just tension](/components/rig/tack-strop.md#the-tack-strut-also-carries-compression-not-just-tension) and [mainsheet, outboard, and rudder fouling — boom-end splitting at the mainsheet-tang bolt](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#boom-end-splitting-at-the-mainsheet-tang-bolt-and-a-construction-fix).[125]
- **An umbrella instead of a topping-lift awning.** Answering a suggestion that a topped-up topping lift could support a sunshade over the open hatch in port, described his own simpler fix: an umbrella that clips directly onto the hatch, good for sun or rain, though he rarely gets the chance to use it for sun. See [Furling system — a second use for the topping lift](/components/rig/furling.md#a-second-use-for-the-topping-lift-readiness-yuloh-clearance-and-a-rain-awning) and [Little Jim — an umbrella instead of a topping-lift awning](/boats/little-jim.md#an-umbrella-instead-of-a-topping-lift-awning).[126] A different owner independently found his own umbrella clamp just as valuable after a first cruise, for the opposite reason (summer heat rather than rain) — see [boats/ironsides.md — a first cruise](/boats/ironsides.md#a-first-cruise-four-days-in-south-puget-sound-july-2009).[127]
- **Finger holes for lifting the sole hatches.** A couple of 1-inch holes drilled straight through his own sole hatches make them easy to lift, a simpler alternative to another builder's turn-button fasteners. See [boats/ironsides.md — turn buttons for the sole hatches](/boats/ironsides.md#turn-buttons-for-the-sole-hatches).[128]
- **Offering his own copy of Don Elliott's Building Manual.** When a newcomer could not reach Don Elliott's estate for a copy of the Building Manual, offered his own — Don having been very protective of his copyright, so the offer was conditional on the newcomer first getting clearance from Don's family. See [Don Elliott — after his death](/people/don-elliott.md#after-his-death) and [Paradox building plans — a July 2009 recurrence](/references/building-plans.md#a-july-2009-recurrence-still-unreachable-through-don-elliotts-own-address).[129]
- **Confirming a grommet/yard mismatch on another builder's new sail, and a sleeve-grommet fix.** Recognized a fellow builder's newly-sewn Sailrite-kit sail as having the same corner-grommet mismatch against the yard that his own sail had shown, suggesting it may simply be how the plan's yard and a Sailrite-cut sail line up rather than a kit fault; had solved his own the other way, by adding two extra grommets partway down the sleeve's side rather than lengthening the yard. See [sailmaking — a grommet/yard lead-angle mismatch, and two independent fixes](/construction/sailmaking.md#a-grommetyard-lead-angle-mismatch-and-two-independent-fixes).[130]
- **A further topping-lift confirmation, for a Taiwan-based builder.** Asked once more whether the topping lift is really necessary, since Matt Layden's own design skips one, gave the same answer as always: not necessary, and Matt does without one, but not something he would want to be without himself. See [furling system — the question recurs once more in 2009](/components/rig/furling.md#the-question-recurs-once-more-in-2009).[131]
- **A simple two-line topping-lift design, given in turn.** Answering the same builder's follow-up request for an actual design, described his own: a rope from each end of the boom joining together and passing through an eye at the masthead, holding the boom in the position it sits at when the sail is hoisted — the only care needed is keeping the yard on the correct side of it when starting to hoist. Pointed to photographs in the group's "Little Jim" folder in the Photos section. See [furling system — the question recurs once more in 2009](/components/rig/furling.md#the-question-recurs-once-more-in-2009).[132]
- **No outboard on Little Jim, and why the trolling motor and yuloh suffice instead.** Carries no outboard at all, seeing no way to mount one clear of the ropes without it tangling with the mainsheet — owners who do fit one simply lower sail and start the motor whenever tacking is needed; carries an electric trolling motor instead, on a bracket beside the seat clear of every rope in flat water, though its propeller lifts clear once the boat heels, and finds the yuloh generally sufficient regardless. See [mainsheet, outboard, and rudder fouling — a 2009 recurrence across four boats, and two new fix ideas](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#a-2009-recurrence-across-four-boats-and-two-new-fix-ideas).[133]
- **A junk-rig doubled mainsheet, weighed against centre-of-pressure placement.** Answering a suggestion that a junk rig's doubled mainsheet — one line to each side of the boom — might solve the outboard-fouling tangle by design, cautioned it would still need to be set well aft regardless, to keep the sail's centre of pressure correctly placed. See [mainsheet, outboard, and rudder fouling — a 2009 recurrence across four boats, and two new fix ideas](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#a-2009-recurrence-across-four-boats-and-two-new-fix-ideas).[134]
- **The tack-strop spacer tube's forked top: a May 1994 plans revision, not a builder choice.** Explained Sheet 9's spacer-tube fitting for a builder puzzled that his own plans tied the tube off with a plain knot while completed boats' photographs showed a forked top instead — a 12mm brass rod spindle bending down and splitting, joined by a 6mm stainless bolt and peened nut to the split top of the strut tube, the tack strop's knot nestling in the resulting "Y"; the mismatch turned out to be a dated Sheet 9 revision between plan editions, not a departure from plans. Recognized the revision at once as exactly what his own famously crumpled tack strut (above) had lacked — the plain-knotted, unforked joint collapses sideways under a reefed sail's load just as his did before being rebuilt. See [tack strop — a May 1994 plans revision explains the forked-vs-plain spacer tube](/components/rig/tack-strop.md#a-may-1994-plans-revision-explains-the-forked-vs-plain-spacer-tube).[135][136]
- **Doubling thinner plywood sheets versus building to the specified single thickness.** Judged doubling two thinner sheets worthwhile only where the specified thickness can't be sourced locally, since a void-free glue joint between the layers takes real care and epoxy; built his own boat in the cheapest marine grade available with no bending trouble at all, using a bow-to-stern nailing sequence with a helper's weight added for the final stretch, and judges the design already far stronger than it needs to be. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a 2009 recurrence: doubling thinner sheets](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2009-recurrence-doubling-thinner-sheets-instead-of-a-single-thick-panel-and-marine-versus-ac-grade-from-a-working-shipwrights-experience).[137]
- **A self-simplification of the forward reefing fitting, tried and reversed.** Once thought he could out-guess Matt's design for the boom's forward reefing attachment and simplified it himself, but a test hoist showed the change would not work, so rebuilt to the stock plan, which has worked perfectly ever since: the tack fixing is in tension whenever full sail is hoisted (the lower strut then carrying no load at all), but goes into compression once reefed, taking on real bending loads as the boom swings around the mast — the same fitting also doing double duty as part of the sail-stowage system. See [furling system — why the forward reefing fitting needs so many parts](/components/rig/furling.md#why-the-forward-reefing-fitting-needs-so-many-parts-tension-when-hoisted-compression-when-reefed).[138]
- **Why the fitting's "Y" split is necessary, not just an improvement.** Corrected a fellow builder's reading that the split was merely a nice-to-have: without it, the joint collapses sideways once the strut goes into compression while sailing reefed, dropping the boom onto the cabin top or, if running, the deck, and making it awkward to tack or gybe — "and can give you a headache." See [furling system — the Y-fitting's purpose confirmed, and the plans' own May 1996 revision](/components/rig/furling.md#the-y-fittings-purpose-confirmed-and-the-plans-own-may-1996-revision).[139]
- **A thought experiment on the tack strop's necessity.** Removing the tack strop entirely and rotating the boom would wind the sail onto the boom while the reefing line pays off the drum, raising the boom up the mast; letting go reverses this under gravity until the boom lands on the deck — illustrating why the strut's support cannot be dispensed with. Confirmed his own independently-attempted simplification of the fitting shares the identical sideways-collapse failure mode as Matt Layden's unmodified original. See [furling system — a follow-up: what removing the tack strop entirely would demonstrate](/components/rig/furling.md#a-follow-up-what-removing-the-tack-strop-entirely-would-demonstrate).[140]
- **Sailing gloves against early-season blisters.** A pair of sailing gloves worn until the hands harden to the furling line is his own standing fix for the rope blisters that come with early-season use — a lesson met independently by a fellow UK builder rigging his own boat for the first time around the same time. See [furling system — drum purchase depends on how much line is already wound on](/components/rig/furling.md#drum-purchase-depends-on-how-much-line-is-already-wound-on-and-a-downhaul-vs-cleated-halyard-trap).[248]
- **Bending the furling rod, in three words.** Asked how builders bend the roller-furling system's bronze rod, answered in the fewest possible words: a strong vice, a long lever, and a big hammer. See [furling system — bending the furling rod: two working methods](/components/rig/furling.md#bending-the-furling-rod-two-working-methods).[254]
- **A boom-end knockdown, and its fix.** On one of his own early outings in too-strong wind, had the topping lift and sheet wrap up with the sail exactly as a fellow owner would later report on "[Ironsides](/boats/ironsides.md)," knocking the boat down with water coming in where the hatch was not quite shut — the boat "shrugged it off" and carried on; cured for good afterward by spacing the boom-end tang further out and setting the topping lift's own join as high as possible for a more vertical pull, judging the knock-down had built his confidence in the boat rather than shaken it. See [furling system — a further boom-end refinement](/components/rig/furling.md#a-further-boom-end-refinement-tang-spacing-and-a-high-topping-lift-join-january-2014).[266]
- **The build order for a wooden box mast.** Answering a beginner's question on whether to glue the mast's pieces together before or after shaping them, set out the order used: shape (taper) each piece first, fit the tapered front and rear pieces to one side while checking the mast is not bent (made easy by the straight rear face), fit the top and bottom plugs and seal/wire the interior, fasten on the second side, then round off the corners. See [mast — reading the mast drawing](/components/rig/mast.md#reading-the-mast-drawing-a-recurring-point-of-confusion).[270]
- **Getting a well-cut sail: hand over the actual spars, not just paper dimensions.** When his own Mk2 sail was made, gave the sailmaker both the yard and the boom to work from directly, who "did an excellent job" as a result — "if your sailmaker doesn't know why you are waving a yard and boom about find another sailmaker." See [Mk2 sail — a yard-taper/sail-cut mismatch, and a longer replacement yard](/components/rig/mk2-sail.md#a-yard-tapersail-cut-mismatch-and-a-longer-replacement-yard).[280]
- **A worked example of the continuous halyard/furling-line trick.** Offered concrete figures for the same continuous-line idea already on record (above): one end of a single rope ties to the yard, the other to the furling drum, serving as both halyard and furling line; 12 m of rope this way leaves only about 2 m (a doubled rope 1 m long) loose in the cabin, and while a 2:1 halyard purchase needs more rope paid out while sailing, it still totals less than rigging two separate lines. See [furling system — a continuous halyard/furling-line innovation](/components/rig/furling.md#alastairs-fuller-walkthrough-and-a-continuous-halyardfurling-line-innovation).[281]
- **Why the mast drawing keeps tripping builders up, diagnosed structurally.** Asked to comment further on the recurring confusion over the mast drawing's three mixed-scale views, judged the real problem to be that Matt tried to fit three separate views into too small a space on one sheet — the fix, in his view, being to redraw it as three separate drawings rather than to read the existing one more carefully. See [mast — reading the mast drawing](/components/rig/mast.md#reading-the-mast-drawing-a-recurring-point-of-confusion).[290]

## Trailering

"Little Jim"'s trailer was designed and built to demanding requirements — launch/recover off a slip without wetting the hubs, and off a flat beach at low tide — generating several never-built concepts (an aircraft-style retracting undercarriage, a suspension-free launching undercarriage, and a tow-ball drop-the-back-end scheme) and the observation that UK trailers favor rubber-in-compression suspension. See [trailering](/operations/trailering.md).

- **Rudder-stock height and the mainsheet.** A rudder stock built to full height as drawn can let the mainsheet catch in the notch between rudder and transom against the top pintle; adjusting the pintle helps, and a flush-with-the-pintle rudder is the better long-term fix. See [rudder and chine experiments — rudder-stock height and mainsheet fouling](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#rudder-stock-height-and-mainsheet-fouling-at-the-transom-notch).
- **On not deviating from the plans, and a rudder-downhaul caution.** A Paradox built simply to sail distances while staying warm and dry needs little deviation from the plans — the one change worth making is a removable roof (concertina, rollover, or soft-top) letting a forward second seat put two people's heads outside at once, easing the two-up problem of one crew always having to stay below. A bungee-cord rudder downhaul needs a built-in forward stop, since the stock rudder has nothing else to prevent it swinging forward to the transom. See [rudder and chine experiments — rudder ballast without a lead casting](/components/rudder.md#rudder-ballast-without-a-lead-casting).
- **UK unbraked-trailer weight limit.** The governing UK legal limit is 750 kg gross, or half the tow vehicle's kerb weight, whichever is less; even so, a loaded trailer without brakes is still hard for one or two people to manhandle. See [trailering — UK trailer weight limits, and whether brakes are necessary](/operations/trailering.md#uk-trailer-weight-limits-and-whether-brakes-are-necessary).
- **Trailer size, matched to the boat.** His own trailer is built as near as possible to "Little Jim"'s own size, with the drawbar sticking out the front and the wheels sticking out the side — a shape he supposed could in principle be made narrower by raising the boat and fitting the wheels underneath instead. See [trailering — Little Jim's actual trailered weight, and two contrasting UK trailer setups](/operations/trailering.md#little-jims-actual-trailered-weight-and-two-contrasting-uk-trailer-setups).[141]
- **Little Jim's actual trailered weight.** At a later weighing, the boat came to 526 kg on the trailer loaded with stores, and the specially built trailer itself — sturdy enough to need no wheels in the water — weighs just over 400 kg, for an all-up weight well over 900 kg; staying under the UK's 750 kg unbraked limit might well be legal, but brakes are worth fitting regardless on anything but a level driveway. See [trailering — Little Jim's actual trailered weight, and two contrasting UK trailer setups](/operations/trailering.md#little-jims-actual-trailered-weight-and-two-contrasting-uk-trailer-setups).[142]
- **The raised rudder blade scraping the upper pintle bracket.** His own boat's paint has scraped off where the bolt heads rub, though the epoxy underneath stays sound — countersinking the bolts deeper would avoid even that; doesn't consider it a real problem. See [rudder and chine experiments — the raised blade scraping against the upper pintle bracket](/components/rudder.md#the-raised-blade-scraping-against-the-upper-pintle-bracket).
- **A custom launching-ramp trailer.** Unable to find a commercial trailer meeting his needs, he designed his own, allowing single-handed launch and recovery with little physical effort at the cost of needing a proper slipway reachable by car; it rides docile behind a 2-litre diesel car, and its controlling design requirement — keeping the trailer's own wheels dry by having it act as a launching ramp — puts considerable upward force on the towball. See [trailering — Al Law's own launching-ramp trailer, and a return to the weight caution](/operations/trailering.md#al-laws-own-launching-ramp-trailer-and-a-return-to-the-weight-caution-october-2010).[238]
- **Bearing-buddy adaptors after a suspension swap.** A factory bearing-buddy kit's own adaptor rings can stop fitting once the suspension units are changed; sawing the end off the new hubs' bearing caps and brazing the buddies directly into them worked as a fix. See [trailering — Faith's trailer failure, and a rebuild from a scrapped van axle](/operations/trailering.md#faiths-trailer-failure-and-a-rebuild-from-a-scrapped-van-axle-july-2011).[251]
- **Dry-launching onto an exposed slipway at low tide.** Deliberately launches onto a slipway left dry by the tide rather than waiting for the water, so as to have a busy, fee-charging slipway entirely to himself until it returns — drop the boat off the trailer, park car and trailer, board, and simply wait, making coffee and reading a book. See [trailering — dry-launching onto an exposed slipway at low tide](/operations/trailering.md#dry-launching-onto-an-exposed-slipway-at-low-tide).[275]
- **Four transverse rollers, and dry brakes after 13 years.** His own trailer uses two rollers to support the boat while travelling and two further aft for winching her on and off, so the wheels never need to enter the water at a slipway and the boat can be launched or recovered even on a dried-out beach — after 13 years the brakes have still never been wet. See [trailering — a 2016 recurrence: transverse versus longitudinal bunks](/operations/trailering.md#a-2016-recurrence-transverse-versus-longitudinal-bunks).[283]
- **A no-tipping platform, and a winch-sizing rule of thumb.** His trailer has no tipping mechanism at all, just a rigid (and heavy) platform built so its back end lines up with the boat's own stern when stowed, putting the wheels at the water's edge and the trailer's own back end over water deep enough to float the boat; recommends sizing any winch several sizes beyond what would seem necessary, his own being rated to 900 kg. See [trailering — a 2016 recurrence: transverse versus longitudinal bunks](/operations/trailering.md#a-2016-recurrence-transverse-versus-longitudinal-bunks).[284]
- **Horizontal-and-vertical roller design restated, with a winch and brake tip.** His own trailer (photographs posted to his personal website) uses a series of horizontal rollers plus a couple of vertical rollers to keep the boat centred — since simplified by removing two forward horizontal rollers found to do little — over-engineered but fifteen years old without significant problems, launching and recovering from a slipway or onto the ground without ever wetting the wheels; recommends a winch several sizes bigger than seems necessary (his own rated at 950 kg) and a braked winch and trailer over the standard ratchet type, since manhandling is much easier braked. See [trailering — a 2017 recurrence](/operations/trailering.md#a-2017-recurrence-a-flatbed-conversion-a-restated-roller-design-and-a-carpeted-bunk-build).[289]
- **Taking the boat off the trailer entirely, on the hard.** Frequently drops and recovers his own Paradox with no ramp involved: pushes the boat off the low, roller-based trailer until it tips and the rudder touches the ground, then drives forward to leave the boat behind, reversing the sequence — rear rollers running on the ground once the trailer is tipped up — to recover her; carries boards to lay down for the rollers if the ground is soft. See [trailering — removing the boat from the trailer entirely](/operations/trailering.md#removing-the-boat-from-the-trailer-entirely-for-storage-or-a-beach-launch-october-2018).[293]
- **Hauling a boat up a beach, and why a purpose-built trailer pays for itself.** Recommends proceeding stern-first when hauling a boat up a beach, since the rudder becomes a problem going forward, or "walking" the boat sideways if no special equipment is on hand; a trailer built to the boat, rather than whatever trailer happened to be at hand, is worth the cost — his own lets him single-handedly launch and recover "Little Jim" in about fifteen minutes, in his late 60s, without breaking a sweat, meaning he goes sailing more often as a result. See [trailering — removing the boat from the trailer entirely](/operations/trailering.md#removing-the-boat-from-the-trailer-entirely-for-storage-or-a-beach-launch-october-2018).[294]
- **A second use for inflatable rollers: lifting, not just sliding.** Laid flat and deflated under a boat already resting on the ground, then inflated, inflatable rollers can lift the whole hull clear of the ground rather than merely roll it along; bearing buddies, while a genuinely good invention, were never fitted to his own trailer since its wheels never go into the water. See [trailering — removing the boat from the trailer entirely](/operations/trailering.md#removing-the-boat-from-the-trailer-entirely-for-storage-or-a-beach-launch-october-2018).[295]

## Sailing performance and weight

- **First weigh-in of a glassed hull.** "Little Jim" weighed **just under 600 lb** with the hull finished and glassed (against a **1,400 lb** design displacement) — the archive's only weigh-in at that build stage — and launched only ~20 kg light of design weight, her launch-day tenderness owing to a force-6 wind rather than to ballast. See [weight, ballast, and displacement](/design/weight-and-ballast.md).
- **Design displacement.** Design displacement in full sailing trim is **1,410 lb (640 kg)**; trailering weight follows by subtracting the crew and **70 litres (70 kg)** of water ballast. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — independent weight and displacement estimates](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#independent-weight-and-displacement-estimates).
- **The 165° self-righting figure.** A graph supplied with the plans (or with the construction articles) shows a correctly ballasted Paradox self-rights from **165 degrees** of heel — independently corroborated by the same figure from a later microcruising.com interview; see [weight, ballast, and displacement](/design/weight-and-ballast.md).
- **Best on the port tack.** "Little Jim"'s best cruising performance came on the **port tack** (sail against the mast), the opposite of the "one tack is always better" pattern other builders reported — see [Little Jim](/boats/little-jim.md). Her fullest passage accounts include a 30-degree-heel overnight anchorage, an 8-mile speed trial averaging 3.7 kn, and a genuine tidal-race transit past St Albans Head on the Poole-to-Portland cruise (see [Little Jim](/boats/little-jim.md)); Matt Layden uses closed-cell foam or a vinyl-covered cushion aboard the original Paradox.
- **A camping-derived sleep system.** A method adapted from camping and diving uses a thin (0.5 in) camping mat with two shaped foam blocks fixed to it at the shoulder/head and hip pressure points — far less bulky than a full mattress and, in practice, very comfortable. See [crew size and cabin fit — sleeping two aboard a Paradox-family cabin](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#sleeping-two-aboard-a-paradox-family-cabin).
- **A Minisail speed comparison.** Sailing fully water-ballasted at close to the 1,400 lb design displacement, "Little Jim" kept pace with a two-up Minisail dinghy in light winds (under Force 2) — see [Little Jim](/boats/little-jim.md#a-lake-outing-and-a-minisail-speed-comparison-september-2003) and [weight, ballast, and displacement — how a Paradox feels underloaded](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#how-a-paradox-feels-underloaded).
- **A firsthand stability account.** "Little Jim" pops back up quickly from a 90° knockdown (against a stability curve suggesting self-righting from 160°); leaning out from the rail only heels her about 30°; and in normal sailing the only significant water shipped was 2-3 litres taken on during a 90° knockdown with the hatch cracked open — otherwise most water aboard comes from wet feet, since the boat can be bailed from inside without opening the hatch. See [self-righting and capsize recovery — firsthand knockdown accounts](/design/self-righting-and-capsize-recovery.md#firsthand-knockdown-accounts).
- **A first Solent crossing, and an 80-90 degree knockdown survived.** In September 2003, "Little Jim" sailed from Poole to Cowes and back — the first Paradox crossing of the Solent on record — riding out a rogue-wave knockdown near Hurst Narrows that put water over the hatch before the boat came up sailing as if nothing had happened. See [Little Jim — a first crossing of the Solent](/boats/little-jim.md#a-first-crossing-of-the-solent-poole-to-cowes-september-2003).
- **What it feels like to be aboard during a knockdown.** The archive's only firsthand account of a sailor's own experience through a 90-degree knockdown: a hand braced against the window frame as the boat went over, and within seconds — before fully registering what had happened — back upright and sailing, seat cushion still in place, with no deliberate restraint needed. The episode was simply "a learning experience" — to avoid sailing in that kind of weather again, rather than any fault of the boat. See [heavy-weather tactics — a firsthand 90-degree knockdown, confirming the point](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#securing-the-crew-during-a-knockdown).
- **An autumn refit.** Fixed two lessons from that crossing — beach-tracked sand and bin dampness, and chafe at the furling-line and tack-line deck fairleads. See [Little Jim — an autumn refit](/boats/little-jim.md#an-autumn-refit-sand-and-damp-stowage-and-chafe-fixes).
- **Sailing hatch-shut is a comfort choice, not a defensive one.** Answering a newcomer's worry that a closed cabin in a gust means anticipating disaster, clarified that closing the hatch is more often simply more comfortable — he sails that way even on calm, sunny days — and that self-righting (rated from as far over as 160°) needs no crew action at all, unlike a Bolger "Birdwatcher"-type hull righted by shifting body weight. See [heavy-weather tactics — whether a closed cabin is a safety risk, or a comfort](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#whether-a-closed-cabin-is-a-safety-risk-or-a-comfort).[143][144]
- **A weathercocking/ballast-trim diagnosis.** Excess weight forward hangs a Paradox nose-to-wind on a tack, while excess weight aft prevents her from coming up into the wind at all — a trim balance that can need rebalancing after gear accumulates over a winter layup. See [sailing characteristics and rig balance — a second round: weathercocking, ballast, and rudder stall](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#a-second-round-weathercocking-ballast-and-rudder-stall).
- **Heavy displacement, not a flawed rig, as the tacking baseline.** Paradox's sluggish coming-about, set against a centerboard dinghy, reflects a heavy-displacement hull with the equivalent of a full-length keel — tacking about as the numbers for that hull type would predict, not a flawed lug rig. See [sailing characteristics — center of effort, rudder stall, and telltale placement as the real tacking variables](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#center-of-effort-rudder-stall-and-telltale-placement-as-the-real-tacking-variables).[145]
- **A cautionary lead-removal experiment.** Adding a season's worth of gear (including a second anchor) without feeling any heavier, then leaving the water ballast tanks empty on one outing produced no apparent handling penalty. Removing 100 lb of lead entirely for the next trip (50 lb from the stern locker, freed up by the new anchor stowage there, and 50 lb from by bulkhead #3) to compensate was disastrous: with the center of gravity moved forward, "Little Jim" developed considerable weather helm and lost her tracking ability entirely — a real-world demonstration of the same forward-weight/weathercocking mechanism [diagnosed above](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#a-second-round-weathercocking-ballast-and-rudder-stall) in the abstract. Restoring the lead to the aft locker fixed it, with more lead planned for removal from bulkhead #3 instead. See [weight, ballast, and displacement](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#real-world-under-ballasted-handling).
- **A mast-lean-out heel test, and the standing 20-degree figure.** A newly launched Paradox feeling tender is a common early impression that eases with time; the question can be settled empirically on a calm day by holding the mast and leaning body weight out over the side — photographed under "Stability & Balance" on Alastair's build website — confirming a heel of about **20 degrees** sailing well close-hauled, the same figure reported repeatedly from actual passages. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — real-world under-ballasted handling](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#real-world-under-ballasted-handling).
- **A forward-weight-shift fix for a stubborn tack, and a quantified 19-tack GPS trial.** During a hard Poole Harbour beat in a full Force 5, ducking into the cabin just before putting the helm over reliably completed tacks that would otherwise stall head to wind — though sculling up a narrow river in the same wind with sail dropped could still leave the boat briefly unable to make any headway at all. A GPS track analyzed afterward produced the fullest quantified tacking-angle trial on record: over 19 tacks (reefed ~50%), an average tacking angle of 130 degrees, ranging from 98 to 150 degrees depending on chop, with heel steady at 20 degrees throughout. See [pointing and tacking-angle expectations](/operations/pointing-and-tacking-angles.md).
- **An occasional tacking quirk, mostly resolved by trim.** "Little Jim" occasionally stuck head-to-wind or failed to come about on launching; experimenting with fore-and-aft trim mostly sorted it, though the effect may partly be down to developing technique rather than the trim itself. Every other point of sail handles the same regardless of trim; gybing stays consistently benign. See [rudder and chine experiments — sudden, total loss of rudder effect mid-tack, and tacking technique](/components/rudder.md#sudden-total-loss-of-rudder-effect-mid-tack-and-tacking-technique).[146][147][148]
- **Naming the rig type.** Worked through why the Paradox rig fits no textbook lug category cleanly — tack forward of the mast without ever dipping around it on a tack, and no boom fastened at the mast as a true balanced lug would carry. See [rig and sail — balanced lug, standing lug, or dipping lug?](/components/rig/index.md#balanced-lug-standing-lug-or-dipping-lug).[149]
- **The long-planned Scilly Isles cruise, sailed as far as Falmouth.** The long-planned joint cruise alongside "Faith" toward the Scilly Isles went ahead in mid-July 2007; unsuitable weather kept both boats from the crossing itself, but the two sailed 140 miles together eastward from Falmouth instead. See [Faith — a 140-mile cruise eastward from Falmouth, with Little Jim](/boats/faith.md#a-140-mile-cruise-eastward-from-falmouth-with-little-jim).
- **A reference height and weight.** Gave his own figures when asked for reference data from Paradox owners: 5'6" and 180 lb, for whom Paradox "fits...like a glove" — squeezing right to the fit's limit being best done in quiet conditions. See [crew size and cabin fit — reference sailor heights and weights](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#reference-sailor-heights-and-weights-and-a-two-up-alternative-the-bolger-micro).[150] Answering a later, unrelated height/weight question, doubted height alone would keep a taller sailor from fitting comfortably, judging suppleness the bigger factor in bad weather; had modified his own boat's opening through to the forward storage so it sits level with the cabin sole, letting the feet stick through if the sleeping space runs short — done for other reasons, not for extra length — and had never heard an owner call the cabin too cramped. See [crew size and cabin fit — a further height/weight question, answered](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#a-further-heightweight-question-answered-december-2009).[151]
- **A flip-up backrest, and two more comfort ideas.** Devised a flip-up backrest for the seating position at bulkhead 4, cut into the seat support to add comfort without affecting the seat's normal use, and remains pleased with it years on. In a "Seating Position" thread, gave a firsthand headroom figure — sitting bolt upright, hair just brushing the hatch cover at about 35.5 in — and recommended lowering the sole over raising the roof for more clearance, since raising the roof has knock-on effects on sail stowage; has since wondered about adding armrests to the backrest's base, and floated an untried small inflatable pool chair as a further comfort option. See [interior bin panels and hinged floor — a flip-up backrest at bulkhead 4](/components/interior.md#a-flip-up-backrest-at-bulkhead-4) and [interior bin panels and hinged floor — seating position, back comfort, and a "mini pilothouse" idea](/components/interior.md#seating-position-back-comfort-and-a-mini-pilothouse-idea).[152][153]
- **A view that Paradox may be "too seaworthy" for two-up sailing, and a Cowes crossing recalled.** Argued the design's real drawback for a couple may be being too seaworthy rather than not seaworthy enough — a hull safe in rough weather can tempt a solo sailor into conditions he would not risk with a companion aboard — illustrated by a Solent crossing made from Poole in storm-force winds, reaching Cowes only minutes ahead of two 40 ft yachts battling across from Lymington, one of three having turned back; judged it a crossing he would not have made carrying anyone else. See [alternatives for a larger sailor — a counterpoint: Paradox as arguably too seaworthy for two-up sailing, and a further design pointer](/similar-boat-designs/alternatives-for-a-larger-sailor.md#a-counterpoint-paradox-as-arguably-too-seaworthy-for-two-up-sailing-and-a-further-design-pointer).[154][155]
- **A partial-reef account, and continued way after masting down.** In a forecast Force 5-7 with only about 2 ft of luff unrolled, still made over 5 knots; on lowering the mast entirely to pass under a bridge, kept moving at 2 knots with no sail set at all — drawing a comparison to Sven Yrvind's own "lifting body" hull claims. Downplayed it afterward as less dramatic than it sounded (over-canvassed at first, settled once reduced) and gave the passage in full — Calshot to Langstone Harbour via the Hayling Island bridge into Chichester Harbour, timed near low water for calmer conditions. See [Little Jim — a partial-reef account in a Force 5-7, and the hull's way after masting down](/boats/little-jim.md#a-partial-reef-account-in-a-force-5-7-and-the-hulls-way-after-masting-down-september-2008).[156][157]
- **A dual-purpose case for ballast spending, and a further reduced lead figure.** Replying to a fellow builder's ballast-sourcing frustration, argued that if lead is going to cost real money anyway, that money may as well buy something with a second, heavier purpose instead — a dismantleable storm anchor of the kind Little Cruiser carries, or a good battery — rather than plain dead weight. Reported his own lead down to about **36 kg (75 lb)**, after weighing "Little Jim" afresh with a folding bicycle now the last big addition of gear — a further drop from the **60 kg** figure (itself counting the battery as lead) given only a month earlier, the two figures left unreconciled alongside this page's other self-inconsistencies. See [boats/ironsides.md — sourcing ballast on a budget](/boats/ironsides.md#sourcing-ballast-on-a-budget-concrete-bricks-against-scarce-pricier-lead) and [weight, ballast, and displacement — ballast material choice](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#ballast-material-choice-lead-against-concrete-iron-and-other-alternatives).[158]
- **A verdict against a pedal generator, using "Little Jim"'s own motor-consumption figures.** Set against a fellow builder's home-built 60-watt pedal generator, gave "Little Jim"'s own small Minn Kota outboard's real consumption — 2 knots at 15 amps on its most efficient setting, 3 knots at 30 amps on the higher one — showing roughly three hours of pedaling would be needed to buy one hour of motoring at the lower speed alone; if spending more on the system at all, would rather put it toward a bigger solar panel and/or wind generator and a bigger battery bank, matching his own standing practice of charging the 80AH battery ashore and calling on the motor only in real need. See [auxiliary outboard power — commercial and DIY pedal generators, and a real-number verdict against the whole idea](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#commercial-and-diy-pedal-generators-and-a-real-number-verdict-against-the-whole-idea).[159]
- **The Beale Park righting-moment test, reported live.** Eleven years before recalling it from memory at the 2020 AYRS talk, the same masthead spring-balance test was reported at the time: "Little Jim" was part of the Dinghy Cruising Association's display at the June 2009 Beale Park Thames Boat Show, where pulling the boat over with a spring balance rigged at the masthead confirmed a correctly ballasted Paradox self-rights from 90 degrees. See [Little Jim — Beale Park Thames Boat Show, and a righting-moment measurement](/boats/little-jim.md#beale-park-thames-boat-show-and-a-righting-moment-measurement-june-2009).[160]
- **A battery already doing double duty as ballast.** Carries an 80Ah AGM battery, about 12 kg, fitted forward of bulkhead 3 in the gap between the water tank and the hull side, powering a small trolling motor good for about 8 miles at 2 knots and charging the boat's other electronics — called on only occasionally, since the yuloh handles most short trips. Questioned on the battery's light weight for its capacity, traced it to having directly replaced one of the boat's own 12 kg lead ballast bricks, a side-by-side size comparison at the time bearing the figure out. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — a 2009 revisit: an in-service battery, and sizing figures](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#a-2009-revisit-an-in-service-battery-and-sizing-figures).[161][162]
- **Trim advice for a fellow builder's railroad-track ballast.** Answering a Patagonian builder's question about installing sections of railroad track (20 kg/metre) obtained as ballast, advised starting the pieces close to bulkhead 3 without fixing them down permanently, since some would likely need repositioning to get the trim right on the first test sails — better too much ballast than too little. See [boats/cachivache.md — ballast: railroad track on hand](/boats/cachivache.md#ballast-railroad-track-on-hand).[163]
- **Questioning Ironsides' boarding step, and the East Coast's plunging-noise puzzle explained.** Asked a fellow builder why he had left the stock rudder boarding step off Ironsides, finding it useful for beach launches, and what he intended to do with a prop-housing mock-up once stowed.[164] Recalling only a guess rather than a measurement, thought the fellow builder's own outboard bracket would ground the hull before the engine itself, putting it under 9 in; separately explained a "plunging" noise sometimes heard aboard "Little Jim" at anchor as the transom bottom sitting proud of the water, ripples slapping the exposed hull — resolved once loading the boat more heavily let her sit deeper — and judged the boarding step's own drag not measurable at 2 knots. See [Little Jim — a transom-slap noise at anchor, explained by trim](/boats/little-jim.md#a-transom-slap-noise-at-anchor-explained-by-trim) and [components/rudder.md — omitting the boarding step for an outboard's sake, and a harder lesson on reboarding](/components/rudder.md#omitting-the-boarding-step-for-an-outboards-sake-and-a-harder-lesson-on-reboarding).[165]
- **Why Yrvind's costlier approach isn't needed, and a six-year water-ingress record.** Set against Sven Yrvind's own foam-composite ocean crossers, argued Paradox — designed for coastal cruising, not ocean crossing — does not need that extra expense: her plywood-and-foam hull is already rugged, stronger than any comparable boat encountered, and buoyant enough not to sink even fully swamped. Across six years of sailing "Little Jim," the only time any real quantity of water came aboard was the same masthead-in-the-water incident recorded above, about two litres; every other wetting was no worse than a splash or spray. See [self-righting and capsize recovery — firsthand knockdown accounts](/design/self-righting-and-capsize-recovery.md#firsthand-knockdown-accounts) and [The "BRIS Paradox" — a 2009 revisit](/boats/bris-paradox.md#a-2009-revisit-still-the-groups-standing-answer).[166] Asked by a fellow owner whether the hatch had been open or closed at the time and what lessons were drawn,[167] the answer pointed to his own build website's "Trip 4" page and a YouTube video documenting the incident, drawing only the general lessons of reefing early and watching the weather — and noting the experience left him confident the boat would look after him even on the occasions he does something silly.[168]
- **A 2010 keel proposal declined, and a further Beale Park retelling.** Rejected a newcomer's proposed bolt-on fin keel — "Little Jim" is already self-righting and self-rescuing, more so than Bolger's Micro, and a keel would only cost her performance — restating the 165-degree stability figure and retelling the 2009 Beale Park righting-moment test for a new audience, this time adding that deliberately shifting weight inside made no noticeable difference to the masthead load, and that nothing stowed aboard moved even with the boat pulled hard over. See [self-righting and capsize recovery — a 2010 proposal for a bolt-on keel, declined](/design/self-righting-and-capsize-recovery.md#a-2010-proposal-for-a-bolt-on-keel-declined) and [Little Jim — Beale Park Thames Boat Show](/boats/little-jim.md#beale-park-thames-boat-show-and-a-righting-moment-measurement-june-2009).[236][237]
- **A further six-year dry record, and a third telling of the tide-race story.** Answering a fellow owner's concern about ventilation in warmer waters, noted "Little Jim" was designed to be sailed off the Florida coast and that an air scoop could easily be added at the deck opening; across six years sailing has had a mugful of water get in via the tiller opening only once, while beached with waves breaking on the transom, and recommends a super-absorbent door-mat inside the hatch to catch water and sand brought aboard on a sailor's feet. Retold his own early tide-race mishap a third time, this time recalling it as one of his first trips "when I didn't have any idea what I was doing," and himself seasick throughout. See [vent baffle — a 2011 warm-water ventilation question, and a six-year dry record](/components/vent-baffle.md#a-2011-warm-water-ventilation-question-and-a-six-year-dry-record) and [heavy-weather tactics — a navigational error into a tide race](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#a-navigational-error-into-a-tide-race-swamping-avoided).[245]
- **Confirms a port-tack windward advantage.** Agreed a fellow owner's impression that a Paradox points better on the port tack, having felt the same himself, and speculated the point at which the sail starts to stall is simply more clearly defined on that tack. See [sailing characteristics — a one-tack-fluttering puzzle, and a contrary experience](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#a-one-tack-fluttering-puzzle-and-a-contrary-experience).[246]
- **Heeled-seating practice.** Answering a newcomer's question about bracing against sliding as the boat heels, described lounging into the leeward corner underway, and standing to lean against the cabin top to relieve aches on a long passage, with a selection of cushions swapped roughly every hour. See [interior bin panels and hinged floor — Quintessential Seat](/components/interior.md#quintessential-seat).[247] Restated the same cushion-rotation practice a year later — five cushions of different construction, changed roughly hourly — while confirming to a prospective builder that either a beanbag chair or a footwell should solve a hatch-closed, knees-bent seating problem, and repeating his own established notched-seat-support/flip-up-backrest technique. See [interior bin panels and hinged floor — a beanbag chair, a footwell, or a low bench for hatch-closed sitting](/components/interior.md#a-beanbag-chair-a-footwell-or-a-low-bench-for-hatch-closed-sitting).[258]
- **A stability comparison against a newly launched US Paradox.** Watching video of a capsize test aboard a newly launched "[Odyssey](/boats/odyssey.md)" — heeled to about 45 degrees under a 240-260 lb load hung from the masthead, well past where he could get his own "Little Jim" to heel by the same method — wondered whether her owner was simply heavier or she needed more ballast; she was already carrying ballast at the plans' upper design level. See [self-righting and capsize recovery — a 2011 comparison](/design/self-righting-and-capsize-recovery.md#a-2011-comparison-a-heavier-hanging-load-needed-to-heel-odyssey-than-little-jim).[249]
- **A canvas-roof, forward-seat proposal for occasional two-up day sailing.** Answering a newcomer's question about sailing two-up, proposed replacing the plywood cabin roof and hatch with canvas and fitting a second seat at bulkhead 3 so a guest could sit forward of the skipper — trading away some ultimate seaworthiness for daysailing comfort, though still judged safer than an open dinghy. See [crew size and cabin fit — a 2011 answer: canvas roof and a second seat for occasional day-sailing two-up](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#a-2011-answer-canvas-roof-and-a-second-seat-for-occasional-day-sailing-two-up).[250]
- **A mock-up cannot reproduce a boat's motion underway, and an alternative to prolonged sitting.** Answering a newcomer weighing a stock-versus-20%-enlarged Paradox by way of a static cardboard mock-up, cautioned that a boat moves constantly once actually sailing, unlike a mock-up sitting still.[259] A further reply offered, from his own long-passage practice, an alternative to sitting for hours at a stretch: standing and resting the elbows on the open hatch, or leaning over the side to watch the water go by. See [building Paradox larger — a 2012 recurrence: the 16.5 ft document revisited, and two fit-driven size questions](/design/larger-paradox.md#a-2012-recurrence-the-165-ft-document-revisited-and-two-fit-driven-size-questions).[260]
- **A cargo-capacity answer, and a two-extra-passenger data point.** Restated the 1,410 lb (640 kg) design displacement for a newcomer asking about weight capacity outright, and broke it down further: with little other gear aboard, 180 kg (400 lb) is lead ballast and 70 kg (150 lb) is water ballast, leaving in theory about 180 kg (400 lb) for stores, anchors, bicycles, and batteries; in practice has carried two extra people alongside his own trim without a noticeable sailing-performance change, though cramped and not sailed far in that condition. Pressed to narrow the answer to occupant weight specifically, clarified there is no separate passenger allowance — a person is handled like any other added weight, brought aboard and then balanced by removing lead ballast to restore the correct design displacement. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — a 2012 cargo-capacity question, and a two-extra-passenger data point](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#a-2012-cargo-capacity-question-and-a-two-extra-passenger-data-point).[262][263]
- **Confirming another owner's tacking-angle estimate, a mid-tack sail-trim technique, and a lee-shore self-rescue story.** Corroborated a fellow owner's GPS-based 90-95 degree tacking-angle estimate as matching his own guess, added his own rule of thumb of about 20 degrees of optimum heel, and described raising or lowering the sail about 6 inches partway through a tack to fine-tune an over- or under-reefed sail. See [pointing and tacking-angle expectations](/operations/pointing-and-tacking-angles.md#gps-course-over-ground-versus-compass-and-apparent-wind-two-ways-to-define-tacking-angle). Also recounted sailing "little Jim" off a lee shore under sail alone, using the rudder dropped onto the mud as a lever and the boarding step to get aboard — see [Little Jim — a lee-shore self-rescue under sail alone](/boats/little-jim.md#a-lee-shore-self-rescue-under-sail-alone-december-2013).[265]
- **The cabin windows, defended.** Answering a newcomer's worry that sitting too close to the glazing risks a lurch smacking a sailor's head, and a follow-up asking whether pushing the windows out to the sheer line would help: found no danger in practice, most of the space under the side deck being storage rather than headroom he would gain by moving the glazing outward, and would rather keep the design as drawn — the side window at the cabin's rear corner is where he rests a shoulder in ordinary sailing, going forward would be harder with the windows moved, and the neat stowage the side decks give the yuloh and furled sail would be lost. See [pilothouse — trunk cabin versus a raised-deck cabin](/components/pilothouse.md#trunk-cabin-versus-a-raised-deck-cabin).[271][272][273]
- **Waves, not the lack of a deep keel, kill windward speed.** Answering a fellow owner's report of making no progress to windward in a 25-knot short chop, judged flat water would likely have let good progress be made, the waves themselves being what kills speed in a small boat like this — and joked that he tries to be a gentleman and therefore resists sailing to windward. See [heavy-weather tactics — why short, steep seas stall windward progress even when reefed](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#why-short-steep-seas-stall-windward-progress-even-when-reefed-2015).[279]
- **Tacking advice for a centered sail, and when to wear round instead.** Answering a builder wondering whether centering the sail would cure the boat's known reluctance to tack from port to starboard, judged it would more likely make the tacking problem worse; the boat can usually be persuaded to fall off onto the starboard tack regardless, the difficulty mostly showing up when not concentrating, needing a bit of speed carried into the tack in choppy water, and in winds over Force 6 better handled by wearing round than by tacking at all. See [sailing characteristics — a one-tack-fluttering puzzle](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#a-one-tack-fluttering-puzzle-and-a-contrary-experience).[282]
- **Riding through breaking waves bow-on.** Kept bow square to an oncoming sea, "Little Jim" rides very comfortably through waves breaking considerably higher than the sheer, taking no water over the deck at all — "I was very impressed" — provided the bow is not let fall off the wind. See [heavy-weather tactics — departing through breaking surf](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#departing-through-breaking-surf-an-outboard-sprint-or-a-kedge-and-haul-and-riding-waves-bow-on).[296]
- **A permanent bow-to-transom line, doing three jobs.** A light line rigged permanently each side, from the bow to a point near the transom, serves as an instant bow rope for tying up to a pontoon, lets the anchor be set from the bow without leaving the cockpit, and gives a harness a clip-on point for the rare occasion of going out on deck at sea — not once needed for that last purpose in twenty years of sailing "Little Jim." Would not go forward at all with the sail up, the boom being as likely as anything to put a sailor over the side; the mast is the main handhold on the rare occasion the foredeck is reached. See [personal safety gear and man-overboard preparedness — lifelines and pulpits](/operations/personal-safety-gear.md#lifelines-and-pulpits-a-permanent-bow-to-transom-line-instead).[297]

## Design philosophy and community role

Alastair was a consistent voice for **designer fidelity** against speculative material/layout changes — objecting on those grounds to both an aluminum Paradox and a lengthened "motorsailer" Paradox, on the reasoning that substituting materials or purpose "throws away the designer's skill" and that a boat for different waters should be designed from scratch (see [aluminum Paradox](/design/ideas/aluminum-construction-idea.md) and [Motorsailer Paradox](/design/ideas/motorsailer-idea.md)).
