The same skepticism met a 2011 revival of the aluminum-Paradox debate's favorite proof-text — Bernard Moitessier's "you can kick a can all down the street" line — with a dry rebuttal that a small wooden barrel, or a plastic drinks bottle, would probably survive a run-over just as well as a can, without that making either "a good design for a sailing craft."[253] The same instinct answered a fellow builder's proposal to replace bulkhead #2's vent trunk with a mast tabernacle: "If it ain't broke, why fix it? It would be easy to change Paradox, a lot harder to improve it." See [Endeavour — a tabernacle mast-step proposal for bulkhead #2, and a mixed reaction](/boats/endeavour.md#a-tabernacle-mast-step-proposal-for-bulkhead-2-and-a-mixed-reaction).[252] The same argument recurred more bluntly in the "Steel?" thread's design-philosophy exchange: a Paradox built in wood/epoxy is what the design *is*; the whole steel idea seemed like "hot air," and anyone serious about it should build one and report back rather than keep arguing — Paradox is "a silk purse in boating terms," and there is no reason to turn it into "a sow's ear." See [aluminum and steel Paradox (speculative) — answering the objection: fog, precedents, and fabrication specifics](/design/ideas/aluminum-construction-idea.md#answering-the-objection-fog-precedents-and-fabrication-specifics). Chine runners work rather than a keel by design, with heeling countered by hull shape and ballast — explained to newcomers with a disc-vs-cup (anemometer) analogy; see [chine runners and windward performance](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md). Recurring newcomer questions on first-boat suitability, tools, scaling, sculling speed, and sanitation ("Bucket & chuck it!") point to the construction articles and the plans; Matt Layden recalled averaging **2 knots** sculling the calm section of the 2003 Everglades Challenge (see [sculling and yuloh technique](/operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md)). A maintained website hosted both a construction-photo gallery and a **20-picture CATIA CAD model** of the Paradox, built during CAD training and added to the group's links area, signed off "Hoping for calm nights." The site's photographs directly shaped other builders' own building sequence and technique choices; its design had simply been borrowed from elsewhere, the only real effort being typing in the correct photo file names.[169] When two other correspondents' advice on the list appeared to conflict, he defused it with a wry closing note that one had given advice "which apparently isn't his personal opinion" and the other advice "with supporting reasoning, which presumably is his personal opinion," leaving everyone free to form their own view without the exchange turning into a confrontation.[170] A Paradox's bilge pump sees little real use: even knocked flat with the hatch cracked open, too little water comes aboard for a portable pump to get a purchase on — a sponge suffices; see [bailing and bilge pumps](/operations/bailing-and-bilge-pumps.md#real-world-need-for-the-bilge-pump). On fit and comfort for very tall sailors, height alone is not decisive — how it is distributed between torso and legs matters more, since a long-torso/short-legs build can find the hatch-shut cabin cramped at a height that poses no problem on a long-legged/short-torso build of the same overall height. A claustrophobia worry answers to a convertible-car analogy: hatch open, it feels like being in the open with only the head exposed; hatch shut, like a car with the top up, a small space most people do not find confining — though seeing a boat in person before deciding is always the better test. See [crew size and cabin fit — a very large sailor's fit: 6'8"/315 lb, and a cardboard-mockup suggestion](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#a-very-large-sailors-fit-68315-lb-and-a-cardboard-mockup-suggestion). A Build Manual's stress on precision in beam lamination can needlessly worry novice builders — a deliberately workboat-finish approach still produces exactly the sailing boat wanted; deck beams laminated from **7 mm strips, steamed before bending**, need only 3 clamps per beam; see [deck and cabin beam lamination](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#the-build-manuals-deck-beam-template-and-laminating-jigs-installment). The first Australian Paradox launch was greeted with "soon it will be truly said that the sun never sets on the Paradox"; see [Aussie Paradox — launch, and a name: "Zero"](/boats/aussie-paradox.md#launch-and-a-name-zero). For tiller-slot watertightness, a simpler fix than either a sealed boot or a redesign is a second rubber baffle disc fixed to the tiller itself, ahead of the transom, to deflect water without blocking airflow. See [transom deck plate — a simpler alternative for the tiller opening: a second rubber baffle disc](/construction/transom-deck-plate.md#a-simpler-alternative-for-the-tiller-opening-a-second-rubber-baffle-disc). Paradox's 70-180 kg ballast range exists to tune trim to whatever stores are loaded for a given trip, not to cover build-weight variance — so a steel-hulled Paradox would still need the same roughly 110 kg (240 lb) of ballast a plywood one does. See [aluminum and steel Paradox (speculative) — ballast placement: independent of hull material](/design/ideas/aluminum-construction-idea.md#ballast-placement-independent-of-hull-material). A real-world ballast data point: "Little Jim" was weighed before launch and lead added to reach the design displacement of 640 kg, with shifting the lead around found to significantly affect handling; the design rule of a pound of lead out for every pound of stores added matters most for long trips, though it went untested over a longest trip of six days. See [aluminum and steel Paradox (speculative) — whether a steel hull frees up any of the lead ballast at all](/design/ideas/aluminum-construction-idea.md#whether-a-steel-hull-frees-up-any-of-the-lead-ballast-at-all). Declined to help pursue a racing handicap rating for Paradox: competitive sailing runs against most of his own reasons for going sailing, since Paradox is "a comfortable home" where arriving quicker, slower, or not at all makes no real difference — a view he underscored by quoting the Water Rat's "messing about in boats" speech from *The Wind in the Willows* in full. See [sailing characteristics — no Portsmouth Yardstick rating on record](/operations/sailing-characteristics.md#no-portsmouth-yardstick-rating-on-record).[171]

How much room the forward stateroom takes up depends on trip length — tight on a two-month trip away from civilization, but far more than needed for a couple of weeks away. "Little Jim"'s stateroom holds only bedding so far (using just two of the side bins), is not entered underway except in calm conditions, and costs no steering control even from the cabin's forward end thanks to the tiller rope running fully around the inside of the boat. The stock central divider in that compartment is "crying out for modification" — a section cut out already for easier battery removal, tool clips considered, and near-total removal possible to open up the space, at the cost of needing to tie everything down more securely. See [interior bin panels and hinged floor](/components/interior.md#forward-compartment-access). Chine runners built as drawn need no sacrificial-rub-rail modification: they give no worries about damage, "built like a tank," and anything capable of real damage to the chines would already be causing much bigger problems than a bit of wet ply — Matt Layden's evident intent was building them strong rather than easily repairable. See [chine runner construction — build order](/chine-runners/construction.md#build-order-runners-integral-with-the-bottom-not-a-sacrificial-add-on). For sliding-ballast-track and windsurfer-rig proposals, building to the plans and sailing the boat first is the sounder approach, since without a standard rig or ballast arrangement to compare against there is no way to know whether a speculative change is really an improvement — internal woodwork besides the bulkheads and tank top comes out in about 10 minutes, so a track system could always be retrofitted later. See [weight, ballast, and displacement — sliding ballast on X-Y tracks](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#sliding-ballast-on-x-y-tracks-speculative).

Answering a Blender-rendered 150%-scale "SUV" concept, doubted it would handle anything like a stock Paradox given the added top weight, and separately wondered aloud how many actual Paradox owners — as against non-owners speculating on the list — would really want the boat bigger. See [stretched Paradox — a 150% "SUV" render, and doubts about handling at that size](/design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md#a-150-suv-render-and-doubts-about-handling-at-that-size) and [stretched Paradox — "Why not scale?": moment of inertia, and getting Matt's plans directly](/design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md#why-not-scale-moment-of-inertia-and-getting-matts-plans-directly).[172][173][174]

A firsthand counterweight to the bow-led-anchor flooding scenario: on one "less sensible" passage, green water repeatedly washed over the whole boat — enough to have swamped an open dinghy — yet only a small quantity of water reached the vent trunk, whose own drain cleared it faster than it came in, with no flooding reaching the stateroom. Three methods exist for anchoring off the bow without leaving the cabin; one in regular practical use doubles as a safety-harness jackline and a ready stern/bow line when coming alongside. See [transom deck plate — bow-led vs. stern-led ground tackle: the rudder-stress and flooding-path case, restated](/construction/transom-deck-plate.md#bow-led-vs-stern-led-ground-tackle-the-rudder-stress-and-flooding-path-case-restated).

On heaving-to: three untested ideas follow from ordinary sailing experience — holding the boom in a fixed position (since the boat weathercocks downwind with the rudder fully raised) to make the hull lie at a chosen angle to the sea; sheeting a couple of feet of sail in tight with the rudder turned up toward the wind, on the reasoning that too little sail would be up to drive through the wind; and deliberately unbalancing the boat before a tack, since an unbalanced Paradox already gives trouble tacking. See [heavy weather tactics — untested heaving-to ideas](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#untested-heaving-to-ideas-boom-position-minimal-sail-and-deliberate-unbalancing).

A wind-against-tide tactical error on an early coastal trip put "Little Jim" close-hauled in a fast tide running against the wind, sending green water over the cabin throughout — handled by the boat with no fuss, and settling a practical case against loose foredeck storage of fuel or an outboard in such conditions. A PVC-tape sheer line washed almost entirely off during that same trip yet not a drop of water got inside the boat, which Alastair credited to the **design** (the cabin hatch seal above all) rather than his own workmanship.[175][176][177] See [heavy weather tactics — a wind-against-tide encounter, and the case against foredeck storage](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#a-wind-against-tide-encounter-and-the-case-against-foredeck-storage).

A firsthand yuloh verdict closes out the "Mainsheet and outboard" canal thread: excellent as a power source once the wind drops, but hard going in any direction but downwind against a real breeze, since the yuloh's thrust acts too far aft to easily stop the bow blowing off. See [inland waterway and canal touring](/merits/inland-waterway-and-canal-touring.md#the-yulohs-real-limit-fine-for-a-lock-or-a-marina-not-for-windward-distance).[178] Separately, a newcomer's plans-request impatience drew a characteristically blunt reply — that impatience this early might mean Paradox, "more of a relaxed sail," is not the right boat — before again pointing him to `microcruising.com` for the plans themselves. See [building plans — source and cost](/references/building-plans.md#source-and-cost).[179]

Answering a prospective builder's cabin-dimension question, Alastair reported 930 mm (about 36.5 in) of clearance under "Little Jim"'s closed hatch, built as close to the plans as possible, and noted the roof could go up or the floor down if more clearance were ever wanted. See [crew size and fit — measured cabin dimensions](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#glen-maxwells-measured-cabin-dimensions).[180] A separate max-pilot-weight/height question drew the judgment that weight is largely irrelevant at stock length, since removable lead ballast can be reduced to compensate, along with "Little Jim"'s own sitting headroom (860 mm/~34 in) and stock sleeping length (1.8 m/~5 ft 11 in, extendable a further 0.8 m/~31 in with a minor mod). See [crew size and fit — pilot weight, height, and stability at stock length](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#pilot-weight-height-and-stability-at-stock-length).[181] A further, 2015 sitting-height question drew the same reassurance restated in practice: comfortably seated inside at 5 ft 6 in, hat included, since heeling under sail calls for ducking down to leeward and sitting up to windward regardless of stature, and since sailing itself is done reclined rather than bolt upright, leaving more headroom in practice than a static measurement would suggest. See [crew size and fit — a 2015 sitting-height question, and a builder-to-be's own trial](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#a-2015-sitting-height-question-and-a-builder-to-bes-own-trial).[274] On long-passage seat comfort, he rotates between a hard foam cushion, a soft foam cushion, and a small inner tube every couple of hours, finding the rotation itself — not any single cushion — is what actually reduces the pain. See [crew size and fit — long-passage seat comfort: cushion practice](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#long-passage-seat-comfort-cushion-practice).[182][183] Built his own Paradox on a deliberate "better too much than too little" epoxy principle, using 2 lots of 25 kg with some left over. See [epoxy brands and chemistry — how much epoxy a build actually uses](/construction/epoxy-brands-and-chemistry.md#how-much-epoxy-a-build-actually-uses).[184] On drinking water in an epoxy-lined tank, his own supplier advised against it, a decision confirmed by sludge found in the tank at the end of the first season; the built-in tank is used as ballast only, filled with sea water from remote launch sites, with drinking water carried separately. See [water tank construction and lining](/construction/water-tank-lining.md).[185] Only one Paradox has ever been known to be resold — "they seem to be keepers." See [secondhand Paradox market — a want-to-buy inquiry from Texas](/operations/secondhand-market.md#a-want-to-buy-inquiry-from-texas).[186] On the transom ventilation port, his own practice keeps the cover on except to load or unload spars, relying on the tiller slot alone for ventilation; he floated a bottom-hinged, line-operated cover design for anyone wanting to open the port at sea, and wondered whether Matt Layden — whose own port opens from the inside — actually sails with the cover off. See [transom deck plate — a 2006 revisiting](/construction/transom-deck-plate.md#a-2006-revisiting-an-inward-opening-cover-a-handle-and-lanyard-or-twin-back-to-back-ports).[187][188][189] On mounting a yuloh, cautioned that an ordinary rowlock risks tangling with the mainsheet and wears the oar enough to need good leathers, though a rowlock socket keeps the designed pin as a fallback option. See [boats/enuf.md](/boats/enuf.md#the-yard-revisited-a-strop-and-sheave-halyard-and-finishing-the-boom).[190][191] "Little Jim"'s flat sleeping area measures 1.9 m by 0.9 m, with roughly another 0.5 m of legroom available from a small modification to the forward access. See [first-boat suitability — build-time figures and difficulty rating](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#build-time-figures-and-difficulty-rating).[192] A follow-up two-person-capacity question drew a direct answer: not really a boat for two under way, since only one person fits on the helm seat, though sleeping two is a "cosy affair, but not impossible" with extra width gained by modifying the storage arrangements, as done on at least one other composite-built boat; a stock Paradox's material needs run to about 10 sheets of plywood and 40-50 litres of epoxy. See [first-boat suitability — the two-person question, answered directly](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#the-two-person-question-answered-directly).[193][194] Disputed a stability caution passed to a large prospective builder by pointing to his own photograph of himself trying to capsize "Little Jim" by hand, citing Matt Layden's own two-up sailing and Hugo Vihlen's 105-day, 5 ft 4 in *Father's Day* crossing as evidence little boats can do more than people expect. See [crew size and fit — pilot weight, height, and stability at stock length](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#pilot-weight-height-and-stability-at-stock-length) and [stretched Paradox](/design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md).[195][196] For road travel, the mast, boom, yard, and sail load through the transom hatch as a tight but workable fit, the mast lashed atop the already-stowed boom — the same rig used passing under low bridges under sail. See [transom deck plate — stowing the mast, boom, yard, and sail through the transom hatch for road travel](/construction/transom-deck-plate.md#stowing-the-mast-boom-yard-and-sail-through-the-transom-hatch-for-road-travel).[197] He has slept aboard between cruising grounds in his own motor cruiser and would consider it in a Paradox, favoring an ordinary residential street over a remote layby after once being woken there by police (though never moved on). See [boats/enuf.md — a carpal-tunnel operation halts progress, and a five-week pause](/boats/enuf.md#a-carpal-tunnel-operation-halts-progress-and-a-five-week-pause).[198] The poor market for unconventional small-boat designs owes more to a racing-influenced marketing effect than to any real performance shortfall. See [secondhand Paradox market — build vs. buy secondhand: a UK newcomer's cost-benefit hesitation](/operations/secondhand-market.md#build-vs-buy-secondhand-a-uk-newcomers-cost-benefit-hesitation).[199][200][201] Judged the paid construction manual useful for a first-timer despite "quite a lot of less useful stuff" in it. See [building plans — a July 2006 recurrence, and a blunt assessment of the paid manual's quality](/references/building-plans.md#a-july-2006-recurrence-and-a-blunt-assessment-of-the-paid-manuals-quality).[202] Agreed with a proposal to narrow and reinforce bulkhead 3 for two-person sleeping, adding only that the bulkhead should be temporarily braced across its top until the hanging knees go in. See [bulkheads — modifying bulkhead 3 to gain room for sleeping two](/components/bulkheads.md#modifying-bulkhead-3-to-gain-room-for-sleeping-two).[203] On a fellow builder's rudder photos, reported that both of them had found the mainsheet dropping into the rudder-stock/transom gap and riding up over the pintle/gudgeon on a broad reach or run, fixed with a small wooden fairlead insert. See [design/rudder.md — rudder-stock height and mainsheet fouling at the transom notch](/components/mainsheet-outboard-fouling.md#rudder-stock-height-and-mainsheet-fouling-at-the-transom-notch).[204] When "Enuf"'s builder announced his departure from the group, Alastair argued he had a duty to keep lurking and offering build advice rather than leave outright, estimating he held some 11% of the world's complete Paradox build knowledge. See [boats/enuf.md](/boats/enuf.md#a-departure-an-outpouring-of-support-and-second-thoughts).[205]

After a rough F5-6 weekend out turned up a leaking water-ballast tank inspection cover — a recurring nuisance, an earlier replacement on the other tank having brought only limited success — decided to abandon water ballast for permanent lead, keeping just 10 kg as water ballast in 2-litre bottles for trim. See [Little Jim — abandoning water ballast for lead, after a leaking tank inspection cover](/boats/little-jim.md#abandoning-water-ballast-for-lead-after-a-leaking-tank-inspection-cover-may-2007).[206]

Weighing in on a small explorer boat then sailing around the UK, judged Paradox capable of a round-Britain attempt with a competent skipper despite not being a racing boat, and offered the archive's clearest one-line characterization of how the boat is actually sailed: "not really the boat for those who enjoy sailing on the edge," since most of the time she sails herself with her skipper along for the ride. See [blue-water suitability — a round-Britain question, and whether Paradox is a dinghy or a micro-yacht](/design/blue-water-suitability.md#a-round-britain-question-and-whether-paradox-is-a-dinghy-or-a-micro-yacht).[207][208]

Answering a fellow builder's proposal to fit the chine-runner blank before the bottom panel goes on rather than after, agreed the order would be much easier to clamp — especially with the blank steamed first — and recalled again finding it impossible to bend full-length runner pieces into position on his own build, having cut them into roughly foot-length sections instead. See [chine runner construction technique — fitting order: blank before or after the bottom panel](/chine-runners/construction.md#fitting-order-blank-before-or-after-the-bottom-panel).[209]

Framed Paradox's advantage over both NOLI and Enigma as the "comfort of confidence" of never needing to survey a drying-out spot for rocks, since the boat's own heavy bottom board is strong enough regardless — recalling Faith drying out squarely on a submerged concrete mooring sinker with no damage found once refloated. See [Faith — drying out on a sunken mooring block during the West Country cruise](/boats/faith.md#drying-out-on-a-sunken-mooring-block-during-the-west-country-cruise).[210]

Recalled two other boats damaged badly enough to be taking on water within a single season, one from drying out on a stone and one from striking a just-submerged post, plus a separate grounding the year before serious enough to force an abandoned cruise and an immediate beaching. See [hull scantlings — the case for over-construction](/design/hull-scantlings.md#the-case-for-over-construction-field-evidence-hazards-and-a-counter-argument).[211]

Confirmed the same beaching behavior himself: an upward force on the rudder, as when beaching, pushes the blade up until it hits the underside of the boat rather than loading the pintles, so bouncing on a beach while drying out or refloating does no harm to the structure despite how it sounds and feels. See [rudder and chine experiments — beaching stern-first: a sleeping-comfort technique, and why it doesn't damage the rudder](/components/rudder.md#beaching-stern-first-a-sleeping-comfort-technique-and-why-it-doesnt-damage-the-rudder).[212]

Answering a newcomer's question about what stops the lowered rudder blade from swinging forward into the transom, reported that his own blade does clonk the transom corner when let down freely, but then moves away enough to allow the tiller's full swing; has never measured the angle it settles at, though the haul-up rope can constrain it to hang shallower. See [rudder and chine experiments — rudder blade stop: clonking the transom corner, and the haul-up rope as a check](/components/rudder.md#rudder-blade-stop-clonking-the-transom-corner-and-the-haul-up-rope-as-a-check).[213]

- **Four years of UK sun on exposed epoxy, and a peeling-paint lesson.** Epoxy-coated all his own interior woodwork, then painted the areas that caught the sun — a job he now regrets, since the paint has peeled steadily, applied on the assumption that unprotected epoxy needed shielding from UV; four years of UK sun has done no obvious damage to the large areas of bare epoxy left exposed, suggesting the original warning was calibrated to stronger (Florida) sunlight; still judges the epoxy coating itself a good, hard-wearing surface. See [epoxy pre-coating technique — interior finish: bare wood, epoxy, varnish, cetol, or paint](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#interior-finish-bare-wood-epoxy-varnish-cetol-or-paint).[214]
- **A primer-temperature paint failure, traced to his own error.** Reported his single-pack paint failure over epoxy was entirely his own doing: an expensive primer formulated specifically to bond to epoxy, which had worked well elsewhere, was applied in the middle of winter, only afterward turning out to carry a 6°C minimum-application-temperature warning on its own tin. See [epoxy pre-coating technique — interior finish: bare wood, epoxy, varnish, cetol, or paint](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#interior-finish-bare-wood-epoxy-varnish-cetol-or-paint).[215]
- **Even less need to paint a Paradox's interior.** With tinted Lexan already supplying UV protection, argued the interior needs painting even less than most boats' — the sun only reaches it while actually under sail, and then only whatever gets past the sailor's own body plugging the hatch opening. See [epoxy pre-coating technique — interior finish: bare wood, epoxy, varnish, cetol, or paint](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#interior-finish-bare-wood-epoxy-varnish-cetol-or-paint).[216]
- **A plywood-cost-and-grade answer, and the plans FAQ's turnaround time.** Restated Matt Layden's plans specification of exterior-grade plywood, hoping the better material pays off in durability against upkeep; separately, on the recurring plans-request FAQ, judged the distributor was probably simply away, and put the usual ordering-to-delivery turnaround at no more than a couple of weeks. See [plywood grade and foam flotation debate — a 2008 recurrence: the cost gap between a "top job" and an economy build](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2008-recurrence-the-cost-gap-between-a-top-job-and-an-economy-build) and [building plans — a second March 2008 recurrence, and delivery-time data points](/references/building-plans.md#a-second-march-2008-recurrence-and-delivery-time-data-points).[217][218][219]
- **On pushing past "sensible."** Framed his own approach to risk plainly: sensible limits need testing occasionally to know where they really lie, or sailing risks turning bland — and every time he has gone beyond sensible into what he calls "stupid," his Paradox has brought him through.[267]

Illustrated a fellow builder's transom-refuelling routine with photographs posted to the group's photo section — captioned "in the middle of the Atlantic" — showing the engine left running and in gear with no safety harness worn, then answered a newcomer weighing gasoline against electric propulsion with concrete consumption figures for his own 28 lb-thrust Minn Kota motor: 3 knots on about 30 amps, or 2 knots on about 15 amps. See [auxiliary outboard power — reaching a transom motor's throttle and fuel from inside the cabin](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#a-side-mounted-motor-and-fuel-sponson-considered-and-rejected-a-three-position-transom-bracket) and [auxiliary outboard power — further field experience with a side-mounted outboard](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#further-field-experience-with-a-side-mounted-outboard).[220][221]

**Corrections and additions to a 2015 group registry.** Answering a newly-built successor to Bill Serjeant's own Paradox registry, supplied several ownership updates from memory: [Faith](/boats/faith.md) had passed from Bill Serjeant to Jim Hall, of Shaftesbury, England; "Enuf," renamed "Minnow" under Robert Biegler's Norwegian ownership and sold on to Bill Serjeant, had since been sold again, to Richard Green. More tentatively recalled, as "vague recollections": [Petite Lady](/boats/petite-lady.md) as built by Hugh Miller and now owned by a "P D Benn"; a Paradox built in Sweden by [Kjell Karlsson](/people/kjell-karlsson.md); and "Raffles," built by [Mark Waters](/people/mark-waters.md), recalled as built in Sacramento, California, against Waters' own firsthand account of building her in Los Angeles. See [references/builders-and-owners-census-2014.md](/references/builders-and-owners-census-2014.md#a-2015-proposal-for-a-group-maintained-update-and-a-short-lived-successor-registry) for the fuller corroboration this drew.[269]

On cold-weather cabin comfort, credited "Little Jim"'s design-standard foam — about two inches lining the sides and deck — with covering flotation, sound-deadening against slapping halyards, and insulation all at once, and reported never having needed a cabin heater even through snow and a night waking to frost on the deck, relying on body heat alone most of the time and, on a genuinely cold evening, a coffee made before bed to warm the cabin through the night. See [cabin heating for cold-weather sailing — a November 2015 continuation](/operations/cabin-heating.md#a-november-2015-continuation-further-heater-detail-and-a-foam-insulation-contrast).[278]

Confirmed from firsthand experience, replying to a calorie-based debate over human-powered propulsion, that rowing (sculling) a Paradox is a genuinely low-energy exercise even for a self-described non-athlete: a 3-mile session averaged just under 2 knots, and the longest continuous session ran 4 hours with room to continue, though trying to push the boat fast tires a rower out quickly. See [sculling and yuloh technique — performance](/operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md#performance) and [auxiliary outboard power — a calorie-based case for human power, and its real-world limits](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#a-calorie-based-case-for-human-power-and-its-real-world-limits).[222]

Closing out the same thread, defended carrying no petrol engine at all beyond an emergency electric motor as a deliberate choice: going without a fallback is what forces sail- and yuloh-handling skill to develop, recalling that Faith's owner's own steep learning curve on Paradox owed something to reaching for the engine whenever conditions turned hard on the wind. Held that even "failing" to get home under sail is no real hardship, just another night aboard rather than mowing the lawn at home. See [Little Jim — a deliberate no-motor choice, and the case for leaving it at home](/boats/little-jim.md#a-deliberate-no-motor-choice-and-the-case-for-leaving-it-at-home).[223]

Continuing the same "Alternative auxiliary power..." thread, gave the fullest public account of his own electric setup: chose a 28 lb-thrust Minn Kota trolling motor after a dealer's demonstrator proved fine at 3 knots, well short of the 54 lb often recommended for a boat this heavy; runs it from an 80Ah AGM deep-cycle battery that also carries the echosounder, GPS, VHF, and phone loads, still about 80% charged after a 3-week cruise, and uses the motor itself only rarely, preferring the yuloh except at one narrow-river launch site with a current not worth fighting by oar. See [auxiliary outboard power — further field experience with a side-mounted outboard](/operations/auxiliary-outboard-power.md#further-field-experience-with-a-side-mounted-outboard).[224][225] On the yuloh itself, reported moving his own pivot hole a further 150mm toward the handle after finding the lower gearing gave too little purchase, settled on an oversized-hole epoxy-and-fibre repair for a worn socket, and found molten candle wax cured a squeak that plain water alone would not. See [Yuloh — building the yuloh itself](/components/yuloh.md#building-the-yuloh-itself-blade-curvature-and-the-pivot-boltsocket).[226]

Encouraging a new California builder just starting his own build, restated the roughly 18-month rule of thumb for a typical build and added a pointed piece of realism: a builder genuinely trying for a near-term launch has effectively answered the question of whether to build a practice boat first, since a compressed schedule leaves no time for one, or for much sailing either. Repeated, to the same newcomer, a caution first raised from firsthand experience during the Scilly Isles cruise: a laminated plywood beam can delaminate within the plywood itself, independent of the epoxy bond, a risk more relevant to free-standing beams than ones bonded directly to a bulkhead. See [boats/wizard-spawn.md](/boats/wizard-spawn.md#encouragement-from-established-builders) and [fastening and gluing — a delamination caution from experience](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#fastening-laminated-ply-panels-a-delamination-caution-from-experience).[227][228]

Continuing the same "p&s" thread the following morning, offered British sailors' own further buoyage shorthand — appending "in the can" to a description to indicate a buoy's flat-topped can shape, as against a pointed conical one. See [building plans — reading the plans](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions) for the fuller mnemonic exchange this fed into.[229]

Weighing a newcomer's stated Marina del Rey-to-Honolulu ambition, judged the boat itself capable of the crossing given the right conditions, the skipper being the weak link, and that asking the question at all was itself evidence of not yet being ready to go; recommended building the boat, sailing progressively longer cruises, and reading firsthand accounts of small-boat ocean crossings in the meantime — in his own reading, most describe a miserable experience even where the passage succeeds. Full thread: [design/blue-water-suitability.md](/design/blue-water-suitability.md#a-2008-marina-del-rey-to-honolulu-plan-and-the-answer-it-drew).[230]

On reading the 2009 *Small Craft Advisor* interview extract published on yrvind.com, confirmed his own understanding of the system had been broadly right and reported not having previously appreciated what a big part the rudder played in it; restated his standing view that chine runners will not work on a multihull, though vortex generators might. See [chine runners: how they work — a rocker/crescent misconception, and the multihull question restated](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md#a-rockercrescent-misconception-and-the-multihull-question-restated).[231]

Restated that same understanding directly to the list some months later, during a debate over whether chine runners could help a PDRacer or a Robbsboat: the lateral resistance comes from the heeled hull acting as a lifting body, with the chine runners themselves "just the icing on the cake," so a hull without the right underwater profile gains nothing from adding them regardless. See [how chine runners work — a December 2009 debate: the Triloboat 16's negative result, and Matt's own explanation restated](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md#a-december-2009-debate-the-triloboat-16s-negative-result-and-matts-own-explanation-restated).[232]

In the same week's "Minimalist sail-inside designs" thread, objected from firsthand experience with Little Jim that a forward-facing cabin opening is exactly where cold wind and colder water come from, then suggested a solid, aircraft-style sliding canopy as an alternative that would also let a flush-decked hull go straight onto a car roof once slid clear. See [a scaled-down "mini" Paradox — Don Elliott's own flush-deck-forward design](/design/ideas/scaled-down-idea.md#don-elliotts-own-flush-deck-forward-design-and-the-fate-of-his-archive).[234][235]

Weighing John Welsford's Fafnir against Paradox for a newcomer, judged the choice comes down to the coastline sailed: his own Paradox suits the south coast of England, where Fafnir's much deeper 2 ft 7 in draft would rule out crossing the bar into smaller rivers, make going ashore awkward, and complicate getting on and off a trailer, while Paradox can often simply be run bow-first onto a beach and stepped off dry-shod. See [similar boat designs — John Welsford's Fafnir](/similar-boat-designs/comparisons.md#john-welsfords-fafnir).[256]

Told in 2015 that starting a build having never seen a real Paradox in person took real nerve — the same position he had been in building the first one in Europe — offered his own dry alternative reading of the compliment: "Or, perhaps, an ignorant one."[277]

Gave the fullest locker-by-locker account on record of how "Little Jim" is actually stowed: emergency spare clothes and boots in the fore-peak; clothes, sleeping bag, air bed, an electric outboard motor, an inflatable tender, a battery charger, and a bicycle in the forward stateroom; a week's food and water under the port floor locker; snacks and coffee to starboard; tools, a spare anchor, chain, and rope in the starboard floor locker; wet-weather gear and spare gas cans forward; a cooker, second anchor, mooring lines, and fenders in the stern locker; and odds and ends on the side shelves — none of it packed to capacity, and by his own account more generous than a conventional dinghy's. Judged crossing an ocean in a boat this size beside the point regardless of the room available: "What is the point in owning a boat like that if you don't put it on the ferry?" Full inventory at [components/interior.md — storage capacity after fit-out](/components/interior.md#storage-capacity-after-fit-out-firsthand-inventories-and-small-stowage-ideas).[286]

Answering a further blue-water question in 2018 — whether a Paradox could manage a 370 nm open-Pacific passage to Lord Howe Island — set out a could/would/should distinction and repeated the same ferry logic in a new context: often asked whether he could sail "Little Jim" across the English Channel to France, and could, but finds crossing open water out of sight of land "very tedious," preferring to sail the French coast instead. Full thread: [design/blue-water-suitability.md — a 2018 test case](/design/blue-water-suitability.md#a-2018-test-case-370-nautical-miles-of-open-pacific-to-lord-howe-island).[292]

# Citations

[1] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 6156 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2009-10-03) — "Re: I just discovered Paradox": full text and citation also at [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#recalling-the-garage-build-for-a-newcomer-october-2009), [operations/first-boat-suitability.md](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#a-one-car-garage-and-a-first-timers-difficulty-question-october-2009), and [construction/build-effort.md](/construction/build-effort.md#workspace); built Little Jim in his garage (a UK garage probably smaller than a US one) on a wheeled dolly for easier access; previous experience was an 8 ft pram dinghy about 25 years earlier; judges a careful worker would have to be very ham-fisted to get it wrong, but it is not a quick build — 18 months for him, several years for others; splendid for living aboard alone for weeks or months, but only good for single-handing; masses of storage, enough that he regularly carries his bicycle aboard. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[2] [AYRS meeting talk](/references/ayrs-meeting-video.md) (YouTube), Alastair — "Amateur Yacht Research Society (AYRS) meeting, May 2020" — May 2020 retrospective talk on "Little Jim" to the Amateur Yacht Research Society; the uploader's title gives his surname as Law. Full detail and citation at [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#a-2020-retrospective-the-ayrs-talk).
[3] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 926 (openboat / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2003-04-23) — "Re: Newbie, sails, Cedar Key Messabout": one-line reply to David C. Smith's question about why he's always hoping for calm nights — "Because I can't get to sleep if the water is all lumpy."
[4] Naming and spelling evidence, collated. Surname in print: "Sailing The Paradox," *DCA Bulletin* 193: 48-49 (Dinghy Cruising Association), signed byline and closing initials "AL"); and *Messing About in Boats* Vol. 09 No. 07, naming "Alistair Law in Little Jim" in a Scillies cruising account. Forename spelling: the sending account's own `From:` display name reads "Alastair" in 321 archive messages against 0 reading "Alistair," and the archive body text runs 1,240 "Alastair" to 44 "Alistair"; both magazine appearances print "Alistair," and the AYRS talk's auto-generated transcript likewise, but an auto-caption is not orthographic evidence. Self-identification is therefore taken as controlling, and the printed "Alistair" recorded as a publication-side variant.
[5] ["Paradox"](/references/dca-paradox-gilchrist.md), *DCA Bulletin* 193: 45-47 (Dinghy Cruising Association), Duncan Gilchrist — a second, independent article in the same *Bulletin* issue as the owner's own appraisal, spelling the forename "Alastair" in running text throughout, against the "Alistair" byline carried two pages later; an editorial photo credit at p. 47 gives the full name "Alastair Law," making this a third independent print attestation of the surname and the only print source to set the forename as the archive does. Source described at [references/dca-paradox-gilchrist.md](/references/dca-paradox-gilchrist.md).
[6] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 2139 (openboat / "Alastair", `paradoxbuilders`, 2004-06-09) — "Re: Budget Building": points to his own "Little Jim" website for build pictures, following his itemised budget breakdown.

[9] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 2785 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2005-06-09) — "Re: Questions regarding Paradox plans.": full text and citation at [references/building-plans.md](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions); forward-stateroom fit-through-the-opening advice and his own central-partition-height reduction.
[10] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 2793 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2005-06-09) — "Re: Questions regarding Paradox plans.": full text and citation at [references/building-plans.md](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions); corrects Derek Clark's misreading of the metal-angle fold direction.
[11] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 2809 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2005-06-10) — "Re: Questions regarding Paradox plans.": full text and citation at [references/building-plans.md](/references/building-plans.md#reading-the-plans-a-first-time-builders-backlog-of-questions); works out a space-saving 18 mm sheet cutting layout for the vent trunk parts, breast hook, and rudder pieces, plus a newspaper-template tip for testing layouts without CAD software.
[12] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) messages 33/38 (Alastair, 2001-07-22/23) — wood delivered Thursday; started laying out panels/bulkheads; bulkhead-#2, transom, side-panel overlaps and layout dimensions (aft base 28 mm, fwd base 115 mm); solutions to nest the parts.
[13] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 2461 (openboat / "Alastair", `paradoxbuilders`, 2004-11-17) — "Winter maintenance": reinforced the rudder's worn leading edge with split, annealed 15 mm copper water pipe, epoxy-riveted on through countersunk holes; confirms to Derek Clark, met in person on a recent outing, that the same 15 mm split copper pipe used between the furling spindle and the deck had given no trouble, being mostly in tension; sailing home the next day (F5 following wind at 4-5 kn, then a broad reach at 3-4 kn, sail 50% reefed) an unexpected gybe badly twisted that pipe's split end, discovered only once trailered; repaired by hammering out the bends and soldering on doublers cut from spare rudder-repair tubing, result stiffer but untested. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."

[27] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4809 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-19) — "There are a lot of dingos about": full text and citation also at [design/modifying-paradox.md](/design/modifying-paradox.md); links a Duckworks home-page article on modifying a design; had himself been tempted to modify while building his own Paradox, but built as drawn, and in hindsight is glad, since every proposed change would have been for the worse. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."

[28] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4813 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-20) — "Re: There are a lot of dingos about": full text and citation also at [design/modifying-paradox.md](/design/modifying-paradox.md) and [boats/noli.md](/boats/noli.md#citations); has no argument with informed modifications, approving of a fellow builder's work on his own Paradox-derived design ("Noli"), and asks whether it yet has a proposed launch date — unanswered on the list; his objection is to changes made without real understanding, having very nearly been such a builder himself; recalls many past list messages proposing changes before the proposer had even seen the plans; observes such builders rarely finish a boat. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[29] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4816 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-20) — "Re: Considering Dingos and Modifying Paradox": full text and citation also at [operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md](/operations/sculling-and-yuloh-technique.md#whether-paradox-can-be-rowed-with-conventional-oars); has considered, but not tried, rowing standing up and facing forward, with oarlock brackets attached to the sides of the hatch opening once open, well forward, to address two yuloh drawbacks — holding the head to wind once it pipes up, and stopping/reversing at close quarters; recounts fitting no motor to "Little Jim" in her first season, coming mainly from a motor-boating background, specifically to force himself to learn to sail, which pushed him to use the yuloh until proficient; now has a small electric trolling motor, stowed awkwardly enough that it is used only once sail or yuloh have failed. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[30] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4828 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-21) — "Re: re charcoal stove": full text and citation also at [operations/cabin-heating.md](/operations/cabin-heating.md#heat-storage-and-insulation-alternatives-to-a-heater); has, on occasion, stuffed a towel into the ventilator to reduce the cooling effect, since Paradox was designed for Florida's climate and may have excess fresh air for cooler climes; already has 2 in of foam and feels no need for more; a sailor used to dinghy or small-yacht sailing may not appreciate how much warmer it can be simply out of the wind — normal sailing clothes have proven comfortable even in weather that went below freezing at night; has never felt the need for a heater. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[31] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4839 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-21) — "Re: Cabin Sole.": full text and citation also at [construction/water-tank-lining.md](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#lead-replacing-leaked-water-ballast-and-a-hinge-material-alternative-to-brass) and [boats/johanna.md](/boats/johanna.md#water-tanks-reconsidered-and-a-rear-sole-plate-question); had originally considered converting the water tanks into general storage but built to plan instead, reasoning hatch covers could always be cut in later; has since replaced the water ballast with lead after leakage, keeping a nearly sealed air space in the tank to preserve flotation when flooded, and would return to water ballast if truly watertight, preferably metal, inspection hatches could be sourced; the rear sole plate's brass hinge picks up a lot of grit, and would be replaced with a webbing hinge if it ever failed; judges a drop-in washboard fine provided it does not compromise the cabin's structure. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[32] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5331 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-08-03) — "Re: Water tanks..again..": full text and citation also at [construction/water-tank-lining.md](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#a-supplemental-insert-idea-and-a-proven-bottled-reserve-nested-in-the-ballast-tank); plastic tanks or bladders are reasonable if shaped and priced right, but nothing is intrinsically wrong with the tanks as designed; his own cheap inspection hatches have not always sealed, letting a little water into the bilges, currently worked around with fixed lead ballast in place of water; has now located a supply of hatches claiming to be air-tight rather than merely weather-proof, and will fit them that winter to return to water ballast. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[33] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5336 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-08-03) — "Re: Water tanks..again..": full text and citation also at [construction/water-tank-lining.md](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#a-supplemental-insert-idea-and-a-proven-bottled-reserve-nested-in-the-ballast-tank); launches and recovers on a trailer every trip, wanting the tanks empty for towing; many launch sites have no tap water, so the tanks often end up filled with sea water, "bang goes the idea of ever drinking from these tanks"; even tap-water fill develops an "interesting layer of slime" inside after a few months; suggests filling 2-litre fizzy-drink bottles and stowing them in the tank before filling the rest with ballast water, fishing bottles out as needed and topping up with sea water — the bottles are free, easily cleaned, or simply replaced. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[34] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3764 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-07-18) — "Re: Plywood Choices": full text and citation at [construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-july-2006-recurrence-and-a-recommendation-for-mdo-plywood). Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[35] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3768 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-07-18) — "Re: Ballast Poll": full text and citation at [design/weight-and-ballast.md](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#ballast). Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[36] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3769 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-07-18) — "Re: New poll for paradoxbuilders": full text and citation at [design/weight-and-ballast.md](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#ballast). Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[37] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4014 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-09-24) — "Re: Ballast access": full text and citation at [design/weight-and-ballast.md](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#reaching-the-ballast-fastenings-after-the-floors-are-in) and [boats/faith.md](/boats/faith.md#ballast-access-and-tank-top-questions); drilled and tapped into the top of his own lead pigs and bolted on a plate anchored into the chine log; not particularly rigid, but shows no sign of movement; recommends leaving ballast unfastened at first, since he and Derek Clark both moved pigs around on early sails to balance the boat. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[38] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3846 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-08-12) — "Re: Ballast": full text and citation at [design/ideas/power-paradox.md](/design/ideas/power-paradox.md#ballast-for-a-motor-only-hull); no numeric answer, but a boat is unlikely to suddenly become unstable from too little ballast; "Enuf" launched significantly underballasted with only increased tenderness to show for it; has twice forgotten his own water ballast, noticing only on deck, not while sailing; advises taking all ballast out and reporting back. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[39] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4109 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-11-30) — "Re: Andrew Graham's Zero": full text and citation at [boats/aussie-paradox.md](/boats/aussie-paradox.md#a-later-modification-an-umbrella-pole-on-deck); asks whether the wooden pole in Andrew Graham's new "Zero" photos holds a parasol. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[40] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4112 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-11-30) — "Re: Andrew Graham's Zero": full text and citation at [boats/aussie-paradox.md](/boats/aussie-paradox.md#a-later-modification-an-umbrella-pole-on-deck); describes his own umbrella clamped to the back edge of the hatch, rarely used sailing but useful at anchor to sit with the head outside while shedding rain, rarely used as a sun shade. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[41] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4114 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-12-01) — "Re: Paradox Transom Height??? Please help!": full text and citation at [construction/boom-end-tack-line-holes.md](/construction/boom-end-tack-line-holes.md#the-transoms-overall-height-drawing-sheet-8); his own transom came out at 516 mm overall; cautions against cutting it to size before the deck beams are fitted. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[42] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4147 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-12-16) — "Re: Cabin Beams.": full text and citation at [boats/johanna.md](/boats/johanna.md#cabin-beam-jig-questions-from-sheet-10) and [construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#cabin-top-and-hatch-cover-beam-bevels-open-questions-from-sheet-10); answers Pete Martin's cabin-beam bevel/notch questions with his own pencilled plan figures (52 mm foremost cabin beam, 75 mm lower aft cabin beam) and freehand angle-grinder bevel technique. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[43] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4188 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-01-20) — "Re: Why not scale?": replying to Jeff UK, has been pondering an alternative arrangement in the forward stateroom to store a Brompton folding bicycle, reasoning it would open up sailing destinations he wouldn't otherwise be tempted by. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[44] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4243 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-02-27) — "Re: Different Cabin Configurations": answers Steve Blair's cabin/hatch redesign questions point by point — removable windows: Little Cruiser has one, Matt evidently didn't think it worth including on Paradox, and can't think when he'd have used one himself, since fine weather is spent outside and foul weather sends the crew in; longer sliding hatch/taller roof: Derek Clark spent months mocking up similar ideas for "Enuf," delaying her launch, before staying with the plans; stove ventilation: the cabin's ventilation is good enough shut up tight, and he stuffs a towel in the vent box on cold nights to cut the airflow; had a long list of design improvements before ever sailing, none of which, in hindsight, would have actually improved the boat; his only kept changes were to storage-bin access, which don't affect sailing. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[45] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5184 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-06-18) — "Re: Ironsides progress": full text and citation also at [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#glassing-the-deck-before-the-cabin-recalled-june-2008) and [boats/ironsides.md](/boats/ironsides.md#fiberglassing-before-the-cabin-a-tire-cushioned-roll-over-and-the-rudder-blades-lead-cast); confirms a tire-cushioned hull roll-over technique, having done the same himself; describes glassing the deck before fitting the cabin, so the glass's edge sits under the cabin structure rather than butting against it — a single sheet of glass cloth stretched over the whole deck, openings cut out only once the epoxy started to cure. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[46] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5200 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-06-21) — "Re: UK hardware supplies": full text and citation also at [boats/faith.md](/boats/faith.md#a-laminated-hatch-beam-that-delaminated-in-the-ply-itself) and [construction/fastening-and-gluing.md](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#fastening-laminated-ply-panels-a-delamination-caution-from-experience); recalls Faith's own forward hatch beam, laminated from ply, delaminating within the plywood itself while cruising together in Scilly. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[47] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5202 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-06-21) — "Re: UK hardware supplies": full text and citation also at [construction/fastening-and-gluing.md](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#copper-nails-for-the-forward-cabins-ply-doublers); about a dozen copper nails, riveted through, fasten the ply doublers forming the bottom of the forward cabin end and the front window's lower edge wherever there is no structure below the deck there. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[48] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4245 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-03-06) — "Re: Time to build and cost?": answers a newcomer's build-time/cost question with a rounded figure — about £2,500 UK and roughly 18 months of fairly continuous evening-and-weekend work with the odd week off — and points to his own "Little Jim" website for the full story. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[49] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4252 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-03-08) — "Re: Time to build and cost?": full text and citation at [construction/build-effort.md](/construction/build-effort.md#citations); the cheapest/quickest way to get a boat matching Steve's requirements is to buy secondhand; Paradox has virtually no interior/fittings to trim for a faster build; his own 18-month build could not have been meaningfully shortened by shortcuts, though an experienced builder in a well-equipped shop might halve the time; building an Enigma from photographs alone was floated as a faster alternative, at the cost of losing refinements a photo can't show. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[50] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4296 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-03-26) — "Re: My shopping list?": full text and citation at [construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md](/construction/plywood-grades-and-foam-flotation.md#a-2007-uk-shopping-list-recurrence-far-eastern-ply-and-epoxy-quantity); used the Far Eastern grade himself, 6mm slightly thin outer plies but otherwise fine; on epoxy quantity, start with a 25 litre drum and buy more once it runs out. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[51] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4305 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-03-30) — "Re: Cabin Fans": full text and citation at [construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#citations); a full pound of microballoons ordered for fairing arrived in a bucket labelled 0.3 kg, with some still left over. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[52] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4421 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-05-04) — "Re: Timber (or lumber!)": full text and citation at [boats/johanna.md](/boats/johanna.md#sourcing-douglas-fir-and-sawn-versus-planed-timber); confirms he made the same planed-timber over-buying mistake himself; sawn is fine, cleat sizing not critical to better than about ±5 mm. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[53] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4425 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-05-05) — "Re: Timber (or lumber!)": full text and citation at [boats/johanna.md](/boats/johanna.md#ring-nail-sizing-and-a-bronze-nail-offer); bought and used the 2.5 in #8 ring nail from Robbins; the plans' quantities are about right, with more of the 1.25 in size left over than any other. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[54] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4432 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-05-08) — "Re: JoHanna Progress": full text and citation at [construction/hull-planking.md](/construction/hull-planking.md#how-thick-does-the-bottom-really-need-to-be); finds something very comforting about a 22 mm thick bottom, especially seeing others floundering around checking for rocks before drying out. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[55] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3843 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-08-11) — "Re: Tiller Shape": full text and citation at [components/rudder.md](/components/rudder.md#tiller-construction-laminated-wood-versus-bent-metal-tube); does not recall how he settled on his own tiller's shape, but suggests bending a length of stiff wire against the rudder stock and checking it clears every internal obstruction as the rudder swings side to side. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[56] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3944 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-09-03) — "Re: Tiller Rope Lock": full text and citation at [operations/self-steering.md](/operations/self-steering.md#locking-the-tiller-rope-and-why-most-builders-dont-bother); made two roughly 3-inch discs, one on a bolt and one on a nut fastened under the side deck, clamping the tiller rope between them. Signed "Hoping for calm nights, Al, Yeovil, England."
[57] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 3949 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-09-03) — "Re: Tiller Rope Lock": full text and citation at [operations/self-steering.md](/operations/self-steering.md#locking-the-tiller-rope-and-why-most-builders-dont-bother); agrees a lock is rarely necessary in normal sailing, since he was relying on friction alone (not a lock) when seen standing up; but in shallow water with the rudder partly raised the tiller line must be held or locked, since large tiller loads can be seen. Signed "Hoping for calm nights, Al, Yeovil, England."
[58] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4015 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-09-24) — "Re: Top to water ballast tank": full text and citation at [construction/water-tank-lining.md](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#fixing-the-tank-tops-in-place) and [boats/faith.md](/boats/faith.md#ballast-access-and-tank-top-questions); coats the tank top's underside with masses of thickened glue, reaching in through the access hatches to spread the squeezed-out excess into a fillet by hand, wearing disposable gloves; no leaks at the tank tops this way, unlike the inspection hatches. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[59] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4018 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-09-26) — "Re: Ballast access": full text and citation at [design/weight-and-ballast.md](/design/weight-and-ballast.md#reaching-the-ballast-fastenings-after-the-floors-are-in) and [boats/faith.md](/boats/faith.md#ballast-access-and-tank-top-questions); suggests 1 in access holes above the bolt heads as an alternative to a removable plank. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[60] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4021 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-10-03) — "Re: Haliyard and Reef lines": full text and citation at [components/rig/index.md](/components/rig/index.md#why-matt-laydens-foot-drag-furling-method-needs-no-extra-hardware) and [people/stephen-yahn.md](/people/stephen-yahn.md); explains Matt Layden keeps tension on both halyard and furling lines with his foot because he uses no topping lift, and any dead-drum device would still need a foot, not just hands, to hold the other line's load; built to the stock plans rather than his own early "improvements," most of which he later found stemmed from not understanding the system; the only two changes he judges genuinely successful are the hatch window and splitting the underfloor locker lids so they open with the seat down; remains unsure whether he will keep his water ballast at all. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[61] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4023 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2006-10-04) — "Re: Haliyard and Reef lines": full text and citation at [construction/water-tank-lining.md](/construction/water-tank-lining.md#fixing-the-tank-tops-in-place) and [people/stephen-yahn.md](/people/stephen-yahn.md); does not drink the ballast water either, having found "interesting slime" in the tank after his first season filling from a tap; now fills from whatever water he is floating in via an electric bilge pump, keeping the option to clean and refill with drinking water for a genuinely long trip. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[62] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4942 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-02-28) — "Re: A few questions.": full text and citation also at [construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md](/construction/deck-and-cabin-beams.md#corner-post-angles-on-sheet-10-the-30-degree-and-14-degree-window-seating-detail), [construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#interior-finish-bare-wood-epoxy-varnish-cetol-or-paint), and [components/interior.md](/components/interior.md#cabin-sole-non-slip-and-kneeling-comfort); recommends leaving deck-beam cleats proud and shaping on assembly of the deck; explains the sheet-10 corner-post angles; recommends a primer that sticks to epoxy, with a cold-weather caution, and rates exterior house paint likely hard-wearing enough for interior use; carpets the floor forward of the feet with a foam sleeping mat and recommends a gardener's kneeling mat. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[63] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4945 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-03-02) — "Re: Hello :o) In new here.": full text and citation also at [components/rig/index.md](/components/rig/index.md#sail-bearing-on-the-mast-and-mast-offset) and [design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md](/design/ideas/stretched-paradox.md#carrying-company-aboard-and-the-practical-cost-of-going-bigger); centering the mast fully would lose the furled sail's ability to self-stow on the side deck and would likely need a hole cut in the rear baffle for the sheet to run fair; points to the group's "Larger Paradox" files-section document, noting the considerable displacement increase for a 5-metre boat and what that means for towing car and trailer capacity; a sailor wanting safe, comfortable coastal cruising for two, especially with a motor, may be better served by a different design. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[64] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4879 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-26) — "Re: Cabin Sole.": full text and citation also at [chine-runners/how-they-work.md](/chine-runners/how-they-work.md#whether-a-flat-bottom-is-essential-to-the-mechanism); replying to a link on catamaran "anti-vortex panels" (`ikarus342000.com/Avorart.htm`), distinguishes them from Paradox's chine runners — the panels are fitted to a cat hull that sails flat, whereas Paradox's own leeway reduces specifically as the hull heels. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[65] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4585 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2007-07-04) — "Re: Hull assembly process": full text and citation at [construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md](/construction/hull-assembly-sequence.md#gluing-up-the-hull-one-sitting-or-several-and-locating-the-bulkheads-with-temporary-pins) and [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#a-wire-nail-tooling-pin-technique-for-hull-assembly); answers Pete Martin's hull-gluing-sequence question, recalling the wire-nail tooling-pin technique used throughout Little Jim's original construction. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[66] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4800 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-17) — "Re: Charcoal Stove": full text and citation also at [operations/cabin-heating.md](/operations/cabin-heating.md#a-charcoal-or-kerosene-can-heater-and-the-transom-cook-stove-alternative); warns not to slice off the chimney when opening the hatch. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[67] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4801 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-17) — "Re: Charcoal Stove": full text and citation also at [operations/cabin-heating.md](/operations/cabin-heating.md#a-charcoal-or-kerosene-can-heater-and-the-transom-cook-stove-alternative); has not overnighted colder than about -2°C, where a sleeping bag alone was enough; boiling a mug of coffee on waking warmed the cabin enough to dress in before the heat dissipated; ventilation supplies a single burner stove even with the hatch shut. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[68] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 4808 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-01-19) — "Re: Charcoal Stove": full text and citation also at [design/modifying-paradox.md](/design/modifying-paradox.md); answers a builder's "what are the main constituent elements" question by reframing it as "why," not "what" — the design solves a specific set of problems, so the lug rig (mechanically simple but conceptually complicated) is integral to the whole and would likely produce "a pig's ear" extracted onto a different hull; states the design's real elements as carrying the crew in comfort and safety, in shallow water, in relatively poor weather, without needing to do anything energetic — and just as capably in deep water and good weather. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[69] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5218 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-06-22) — "Re: Pricy bronze.": full text and citation also at [construction/fastening-and-gluing.md](/construction/fastening-and-gluing.md#bronze-ring-nail-cost-and-nails-versus-wood-screws); removing screws would cause about as much damage as pulling a barbed nail, since every joint is already well glued and glassed; the way to get a ring nail out, if it must come out, is to grind off the head, separate the joint, and cut the nail off flush; nails are cheaper, quicker, and more satisfying to install. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[70] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5309 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-07-17) — "Re: Cabin": full text and citation at [construction/window-glazing-materials.md](/construction/window-glazing-materials.md#cabin-condensation-a-drip-rail-a-moulded-gutter-and-window-drain-holes); judged the plans' thin drip-rail strip too vulnerable and moulded a guttering from thickened epoxy instead; drilled a 4mm hole through the lower rear corner of each side window to let condensation drain away. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[71] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5313 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-07-21) — "Re: How to attach an anchor to Paradox": full text and citation at [operations/ground-tackle.md](/operations/ground-tackle.md#attaching-ground-tackle-at-the-bow-cleats-a-stem-u-bolt-and-chafe-protection); the cleats should be plenty strong if built to plan; describes the hose-pipe chafe sleeve and hole-between-the-bolts routing; the stem block needs no further reinforcing for a U-bolt. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[72] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5316 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-07-22) — "Re: How to attach an anchor to Paradox": full text and citation at [operations/ground-tackle.md](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-cabin-fed-bucket-routine-and-treating-any-system-as-optional); whatever anchoring system is used is an option, not a restrictive solution; describes a mooring-pickup routine with a snap-hook bow line and a rear-cleat-doubled casting-off line. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[73] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5318 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-07-24) — "Re: Heaving to": full text and citation also at [operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md](/operations/heavy-weather-tactics.md#a-2008-recurrence-heaving-to-without-a-jib); answers a newcomer's heaving-to question — in a Force 4 aboard Little Jim, lifting the rudder and sheeting in tight leaves the boat lying head to wind, drifting astern at about 1 kt; reducing sail lets the bow fall off further; dropping sail completely leaves her lying nearly broadside; has been told sailing a Paradox is unlike other boats but cannot judge, having had little small-boat experience before Little Jim; learned to sail her by an old sailing friend's advice, "point the boat where you want to go and pull in the sheet until you are moving." Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[74] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5325 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Alastair, `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-07-29) — "Re: Vent Trunk": full text and citation also at [components/vent-box.md](/components/vent-box.md#access-to-the-vent-box-a-removable-side-panel-versus-gluing-it-in-place); considered leaving his own starboard vent-trunk panel removable but glued it on in the end, would have sealed it with the same material used on the windows had he left it removable; has never felt the need to remove it, but it would be easy enough to cut an access panel in it if ever needed. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[75] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message ~5828 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, c. 2008-12-13/14) — "Re: STAYS": full text and citation also at [components/rig/mast.md](/components/rig/mast.md#stepping-the-mast-a-first-timers-question-answered), [people/rodney-cannon.md](/people/rodney-cannon.md), [boats/wizard-spawn.md](/boats/wizard-spawn.md#learning-how-the-mast-is-actually-stepped), and [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#answering-a-first-timers-mast-stepping-question-and-a-newcomers-cluster-of-living-aboard-questions-december-2008); not itself present in the archive as its own file — an apparent numbering gap between a previous message (5827) and a later reply (5829) quoting it in full, the only record of it; gives an eight-step mast-stepping routine (lay the mast along the cabin roof, climb onto the foredeck, swing it vertical, poke the foot through the deck, wiggle it to seat, climb carefully back off, then move on to something less exciting like making coffee), judged almost impossible to get wrong; once the sail is hoisted, the halyard's own downward tension (reinforced indirectly by the furling line) holds the mast firmly in its step, which has never shown the least sign of wanting to move on "Little Jim"; recalls a different builder once making his own mast step fit so well that he had trouble getting the mast back out. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England" (quoted in full within Rodney Cannon's reply, message 5829).
[76] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 5833 (macarmody / "michael", `paradoxbuilders`, 2008-12-19) — "Re: Regarding some of the mechanics of living and working on board a Paradox": full text and citation also at [operations/ground-tackle.md](/operations/ground-tackle.md#a-third-owners-three-anchor-stowage-and-a-permanent-bow-line-launch-method), [operations/provisioning-and-water.md](/operations/provisioning-and-water.md#cooking-in-a-minimal-galley-gimballed-single-burner-stove-no-dedicated-worktop), [operations/head-and-sanitation.md](/operations/head-and-sanitation.md#informal-practice-bucket-and-chuck-it-not-a-fitted-head), [operations/crew-size-and-fit.md](/operations/crew-size-and-fit.md#long-passage-seat-comfort-cushion-practice), and [boats/little-jim.md](/boats/little-jim.md#answering-a-first-timers-mast-stepping-question-and-a-newcomers-cluster-of-living-aboard-questions-december-2008); a thanks-only reply quoting in full a prior message (message ~5832, likewise not itself present in the archive, recovered only via this quotation) that answers a newcomer's cluster of living-aboard questions: three anchors (a 5 kg Bruce, a 10 lb Fisherman, a folding grapnel) all launched from the cabin onto a permanent bow line; cooking seated, twisting round or kneeling on the floor; standing and leaning over the side for the heads; a rotation of cushions for long-passage seat comfort. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England" (quoted within "michael"'s reply).
[77] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 6140 (OpenBoat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2009-08-11) — "Re: Lower rudder pintle": full text and citation also at [components/rudder.md](/components/rudder.md#fitting-the-lower-pintle-without-drilling-the-rudder-stock) and [boats/enuf.md](/boats/enuf.md#a-lower-rudder-pintle-problem-2009); had a related problem — his own pintle came loose as the bond with the epoxy failed, though water did not penetrate the wood since the hole had been drilled oversize and lined with epoxy; made saw cuts into the pintle and roughened its surface before regluing, and will make this part of his regular winter inspection henceforth; suspects the lower pintle takes a lot of load, especially grounding, and that a side push loads the joints between the plywood laminates, which may separate if not well made at the factory; suggests a few flush copper rivets through the stock might help. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
[78] [Paradox builders mailing list](/references/mailing-list.md) message 6179 (openboat / "alopenboat" / Al, `paradoxbuilders`, 2009-10-12) — "Re: Biggest challenges of building Paradox": full text and citation also at [operations/first-boat-suitability.md](/operations/first-boat-suitability.md#an-itemized-list-of-first-timer-worries-and-why-mistakes-are-recoverable), [construction/stem-notching.md](/construction/stem-notching.md#shaping-the-stem-timber-an-angle-grinder), [construction/scarf-joints.md](/construction/scarf-joints.md#an-angle-grinder-method-ganging-multiple-panels-for-one-cut), and [construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md](/construction/epoxy-coating-technique.md#an-electric-stirrer-for-continuous-large-panel-work); answers a newcomer's whole itemized list of feared build steps point by point from his own build of Little Jim — the deck-beam jig, panel layout, stem shaping, scarfing, chine-log lamination, epoxy mixing for large panels, and the lower rudder gudgeon (made from a lump of Tufnol) — closing that none of it is so demanding that a pause of months does the hull any harm. Signed "Al, Yeovil, England."
